## Page 1


QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY AND SPLIT GENERATION IN
LAGRANGIAN FLOER THEORY
MOHAMMED ABOUZAID, KENJI FUKAYA, YONG-GEUN OH, HIROSHI OHTA, KAORU
ONO
Abstract. Given a finite collection of Lagrangian submanifolds L in a com-
pact symplectic manifold X, we construct a cyclic, filtered, strictly unital
curved A∞category L and develop Floer theory of closed-open maps and
open-closed maps. Using them, we prove that, whenever the map from the
quantum cohomology of X to the Hochschild cohomology of the Fukaya cate-
gory L with objects L is injective, the following consequences follow: (1) any
other Lagrangian submanifold equipped with a weak bounding cochain lies
in the category split-generated by L , and (2) the Hochschild homology and
cohomology of the Fukaya category are isomorphic to quantum cohomology.
In the exact case a similar result was obtained in [Ab]. We also provide some
applications.
Date: Revision June 10 2026.
Key words and phrases. Floer cohomology, weakly unobstructed Lagrangian submanifolds,
quantum cohomology, Hochschild homology, Hochschild cohomology, Potential function.

QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY AND SPLIT GENERATION

1. Introduction
In [FOOO1, FOOO2], the last four authors studied the Floer theory of embedded
Lagrangians L in a general closed symplectic manifold (X, ω). One of their main
results takes the following form in a simplified setting: if L is equipped with a Spin
structure then Floer cohomology group HF ∗(L, b) is defined for each element
b ∈c
M(L; Λ0) ⊂Hodd(L; Λ0)
(1.1)
of the set of bounding cochains12 associated to L, an ω-compatible almost complex
structure J and perturbations, where Λ0 denotes the Novikov ring over a field
of characteristic 0 (see the discussion of notation in Section 2). The basic idea
for understanding the space of bounding cochains is that the moduli space of J-
holomorphic disks in M with boundary on L, equipped with one boundary marked
point, defines an even dimensional cochain on L which obstructs the equation d2 = 0
when considering the Floer differential. The space Hodd(L; Λ0) parametrises ways
of deforming the Floer differential, and the space of bounding cochains is the subset
of this parameter space consisting of choices for which the resulting obstruction
vanishes, yielding a well-defined cohomology group.
The Floer cohomology group was shown to be the underlying graded vector space
of an A∞algebra, and an A∞bi-module CF ∗((L0, b0), (L1, b1)) with homology
HF ∗((L0, b0), (L1, b1))
(1.2)
was associated to each pair of Lagrangians equipped with bounding cochains.3
The main goal in [FOOO1, FOOO2] was the study of applications of Floer co-
homology to symplectic topology and the mirror symmetry, so the focus was to
formulate and prove its invariance properties, as well as to develop computational
methods. Among aspects of the theory which were left for subsequent work was the
construction of a category, usually referred to as the Fukaya category, whose objects
are Lagrangians equipped with (weak) bounding cochains, and whose morphism
spaces are given by Equation (1.2). The construction of an A∞algebra associated
to an embedded Lagrangian was generalized by Akaho and Joyce [AJ] to the case of
immersed Lagrangians, relying the foundations of [FOOO1, FOOO2]. In case one
considers a finite collection of transversely intersecting Lagrangian submanifolds,
the construction of an A∞category is a consequence of this generalization, and one
can also extract it from the work of the second author [Fu3].
Starting from [FOOO3], the last four authors have rebuilt the foundations of
Lagrangian Floer theory, replacing the use of singular cochains in [FOOO1, FOOO2]
by differential forms. While largely motivated by the use of averaging arguments
with respect to Hamiltonian torus actions in [FOOO3], the outcome is a simplified
construction of Floer homology, ultimately because the chain-level functoriality of
pushforward and pullback maps, which is used to construct operations in Floer
theory, is easier to establish in the de Rham than in the singular theory.
This paper constructs the A∞category of Lagrangians using the de Rham model,
and establishes a fundamental generation criterion which is used to compute it,

equivalence is independent of J and perturbations.
2We include the case when b is a weak bounding cochain, that is, the case Maurer-Cartan

M. ABOUZAID, K. FUKAYA, Y.-G. OH, H. OHTA, K. ONO
and which has its origin in the first author’s work [Ab] studying Floer theory in the
technically much simpler setting of exact Lagrangians. Later in this introduction,
we shall give a precise statement of the generation criterion, but it is useful at this
stage to comment about what it means to compute a category: naively, this would
entail a list of all (quasi)-isomorphism classes of objects, of the cohomology group
of morphism spaces between them, and of all compositions. Unfortunately, such a
task is in general hopeless even for problems arising in linear algebra [Dro].
Instead, the sense in which we compute the A∞categories arising from La-
grangian Floer theory is that we establish conditions under which we can identify
a fully faithful embedding in a category of modules over some A∞algebra, i.e. one
can assign to each Lagrangian equipped with a bounding cochain a module over
this algebra, and an isomorphism from the Lagrangian Floer cohomology for pairs
to the space of morphisms between the corresponding modules, in such a way that
compositions agree. In fact, the result we prove establishes both that the algebra is
the A∞algebra associated to a finite collection of Lagrangians, and that the mod-
ules we obtain are perfect, i.e. finitely built via extensions and retractions from
free ones. Once an embedding in a category of modules has been established, one
can translate (some) questions about Lagrangians to questions about modules, for
which tools of representation theory or algebraic geometry can be used. For exam-
ple, our result is applicable to all compact toric manifolds (see Theorem 1.20), and
we expect that this will be used to prove the appropriate version of Homological
Mirror Symmetry in this context. We also discuss some applications to quantitative
symplectic topology in Section 32.
In the remainder of this introduction, we give a precise statement of the main
results of the paper, compare our results with related ones in the literature, and
finally give a summary of the results established in each section.
Summary of the results. As we explained earlier, the most straightforward con-
structions in Lagrangian Floer theory in general yield obstructed complexes. It has
been proved to be beneficial to consider deformations and twists of Floer complexes
parametrised by geometric data on the ambient symplectic manifold, because this
makes it more likely that one can find an unobstructed Floer complex. To this end,
we fix for the remainder of this paper a pair of cohomology classes
(b, st) ∈Heven(X; Λ0) × H2(X; Z2),
(1.3)
called the bulk and background classes, which can be thought of as respectively
parametrising ways of changing the area and sign contributions of moduli spaces
of holomorphic curves in Floer theory. More precisely we fix a differential form
representing b.4 Here Λ0 is the Novikov ring (See (2.1).) over the ground ring F
(which is R or C in this paper.)

datum determines an orientation of the moduli spaces of holomorphic curves with

Let L be a finite collection consisting of the pairs (L, b),5 with L ∈L , and b a
bounding cochain. We denote by b the pair (b, {b}) where b is the bulk class and
{b} is the set of weak bounding cochains of various L ∈L . As discussed earlier in
the introduction, previous work has indicated that there is an A∞category with
objects elements of L, with morphisms between pairs of objects given by the Floer
cochain complexes. In Sections 9-14 we construct this category, which we denote
Lb, and establish some of its basic properties.
More precisely, the outcome of
Section 12 is summarised as follows:
Theorem 1.1. The category Lb is a cyclic, filtered, strictly unital curved A∞
category over the Novikov ring Λ0, in which every object is weakly curvature free,
and such that the underlying graded module of morphisms between objects is given
by:
(i) the free Λ0 modules generated by the intersection point if the underlying
Lagrangians are distinct,
(ii) the de Rham cohomology of the L with coefficient in Λ0 if both objects are
supported on L. (See Remark 1.2.)
The independence of Lb of the choices involved (including an almost complex
structure) is stated as Theorem 12.11.
A few words of clarification are in order: the fact that every object is weakly
curvature free is simply a restatement of the fact that all objects that we consider are
equipped with weak bounding cochains. The statement that the category is filtered
is a basic consequence of the fact that holomorphic curves have positive area, and
this is a feature of the category which is largely independent of the choice of model
used to construct it. On the other hand, the statement that we construct a category
which is simultaneously cyclic and strictly unital depends very strongly on our use
5We call the triple θL, relative spin structure of L and a weak bounding cochain b to be a

de Rham theory: the cyclicity statement amounts to a strict implementation of the
compatibility between the operations which we construct with Poincar´e duality,
and the unitality statement asserts that all higher A∞operations identically vanish
when one of the inputs is the unit.
Even though we expect that there will be
alternative approaches to our other main results which do not depend on these
two properties, we rely very heavily on them in later constructions, and it seems
essentially impossible to construct the operation which is cyclic and strictly unital
directly via geometry on the singular chain complex, that was used in [FOOO1,
FOOO2].
Remark 1.2.
(1) For the purpose of studying bounding cochains which contain an element
of H1(L; Λ0) with non-vanishing leading order term ∈H1(L; Λ0/Λ+) =

(2) We include the case an element L of L is immersed.
We assume L is
self-transversal in the following sense. We identify L to a pair (˜L, iL) of a
smooth manifold ˜L and an immersion iL : ˜L →X. We require iL to be
self-transversal, that is, the intersection of (iL, iL) : ˜L × ˜L →X × X with
the diagonal ∼= X ⊂X ×X, is transversal outside the image of the diagonal
embedding ˜L ⊂˜L × ˜L. In case L is immersed the morphism space from L
to itself is H(˜L ×X ˜L; Λ0), that is, the direct sum of H(˜L; Λ0) and the free
Λ0 module with two generators for each self-intersection. See [AJ, Fu3].
(3) Even if we start from a finite set of relatively spin Lagrangians which are not
necessarily assumed to be weakly unobstructed (i.e., without weak bound-
ing cochains b), we can still construct a cyclic filtered strictly unital curved
A∞category over Λ0. The same construction in the current paper works
up to Section 10. Then we skip the process in Section 11 and can move
to Section 12 to obtain curved structure. Here we incorporate tad pole to
construct the canonical model for the curved A∞category. See [FOOO1,
Subsection 5.4.4].
Remark 1.3. We expect that it is possible to equip the singular chain model of
the category L with the structure of a proper Calabi-Yau category, as introduced by
Kontsevich and Soibelman [KS3], which is a homotopically invariant version of the
notion of a cyclic A∞category. It is proved in [KS3, Theorem 10.2.2] that one can
apply formal algebraic constructions to replace a proper Calabi-Yau category with
homology unit over a field of characteristic 0 by an equivalent strictly unital cyclic
category in the case curvature is 0. To the best knowledge of ours, the same kind of
statement is not considered in the literature for the curved case and/or in the case

of a filtered A∞category over Λ0 coefficients. We do not try to prove it7 but use
more geometry to prove Theorem 1.1. Namely we construct a curved homotopically
unital and cyclic filtered A∞category and use algebraic constructions to replace it
by an equivalent curved strictly unital and cyclic filtered A∞category.
Remark 1.4. Using de Rham model we can construct:
(1) a curved unital and cyclic filtered A∞algebra associated to a single em-
bedded Lagrangian submanifold,
(2) a curved cyclic filtered A∞category associated to a finite set of immersed
Lagrangian submanifolds,
(3) a curved strictly unital filtered A∞category associated to a finite set of
immersed Lagrangian submanifolds.
We do not know how to perform the construction as (1), (2), (3) above in the
chain model other than de Rham model, (such as singular homology or Morse
homology and etc.) We can not realize (2) and (3) simultaneously on de Rham
chain complex directly by a geometric construction. However we can realize (2)
and (3) simultaneously by combining it with algebraic constructions.

Remark 1.5. The motivation for the terminology we use is that bpb is a map from
an invariant associated to the open sector controlled by holomorphic curves with
Lagrangian boundary conditions, to the closed sector, in which only curves without
boundary are considered. We shall later use the term closed-open map to refer to
a map in the other direction.
Remark 1.6. The Hochschild homology group HH∗(Lb, Lb) (over Novikov field
Λ) can be defined without reference to the decomposition into potential values, in
which case Equation (1.8) becomes the consequence of a computation. Since we do
not need such a result, we do not prove it in this paper.

Theorems 1.10 and 1.13 and while the Λ coefficient is used in Corollary 1.11 and
Theorems 1.7, 1.14.
As proved by Ganatra in the exact setting [Ga1], the assumptions of Theorem
1.7 in addition imply that the category Lλ is smooth in the sense of [KS3], i.e.
that the diagonal bi-module is quasi-isomorphic to a summand of a perfect bi-
module. In Floer theory, and using pseudo-holomorphic quilts [MWW],[Fu3] to
relate Lagrangians in the product symplectic manifold (X × X, −ω ⊕ω) with bi-
modules. In fact a filtered A∞bi-functor from L × L to a filtered A∞category
whose object set is L × L is constructed in [Fu3].
We will use it to prove the
following.
Theorem 1.16. In the situation of Theorem 1.7 the filtered A∞category Lb ⊗Λ
is cohomologically smooth in the sense of [KS3].10
We remark that the filtered A∞category Lb ⊗Λ is always proper, that is, the
morphism space is finite dimensional. We will prove Theorem 1.16 in Section 26.
Another application of operator p and Theorem 1.14 is the next result which is
related to a conjecture by Kontsevitch-Soibelman [KS1, Conjecture 9.2].
Theorem 1.17. In the situation of Theorem 1.7 there exists a canonical isomor-
phism
HC∗(Lb, Lb)/(un) ∼= H(X; Λ) ⊗Λ[[u]]/(un).
Here HC∗(Lb, Lb) is the negative cyclic homology over Λ, which is a Λ[[u]] mod-
ule. Similar results for positive and periodic cyclic homologies also hold.
Remark 1.18. Kaledin [Kal] proved the conclusion of Theorem 1.17 under the
assumption that the DG category is cohomologically smooth and proper, is defined
over a field of characteristic 0, and is Z graded. So in case Lb is Z graded, Theorem
1.17 may follow from Theorem 1.16.11
We do not know a similar result in the
literature in the case when A∞category is only Z2 graded.

The above results complete the general Floer theoretic package which this paper
constructs. In order to illustrate some applications of this work, we consider the case
when X is a compact toric manifold, possibly equipped with a bulk deformation.
In [FOOO3, FOOO4, FOOO6], the last four authors studied the Floer theory of
Lagrangian tori which arise as fibres of the moment map, and extracted from the
moduli spaces of holomorphic disks of Maslov index 2, with boundary on such fibers,
a potential function on the space of pairs (L, b) consisting of a fibre, equipped with
a first cohomology class b. The relationship with Floer theory is that the critical
points of this function are exactly the pairs (L, b) for which the Floer cohomology
HF ∗((L, b), (L, b); Λ) is nontrivial, and the results of [FOOO4] imply that the set
of critical values is finite; this yields a finite collection L of such pairs (L, b), which
define a non-trivial category Lb.
In [FOOO6], the last four authors proved that the quantum cohomology of a
toric manifold can be recovered from the potential function on the space of fibers
equipped with bounding cochains; in fact, only the formal expansion of this function
near the critical points is required. Given the relationship between the potential
function and Floer cohomology, this suggests that quantum cohomology can be
recovered from the category Lb; this is what we prove in Section 28:
Theorem 1.20. Let (X, ω) be a compact toric manifold equipped with the trivial
background class and an arbitrary bulk class b ∈Heven(X; ΛC
0 ). Let L be the set of
all pairs (L, b) where L is a torus orbit of X and b ∈H1(L; ΛC
0 )/2π√−1H1(L; Z),
such that the Floer cohomology HF((L, (b, b)), (L, (b, b)); ΛC) does not vanish. Then
the map
bpb : HH∗(Lb, Lb; ΛC) →QH∗
b(X; ΛC)
is surjective.
Remark 1.21. In this paper we take and fix bulk class b and bounding cochains
{b}. So hereafter we omit the symbol b from the notation Lb, ˆpb, ˆqb sometimes.
Related results. Many of the results of this paper were announced in 2011–12;
we apologize for the long delay in the appearance of a publicly available text. In
the meantime, several related results have appeared:
• In [Sh1], Sheridan proved a version of Theorem 1.8 for monotone La-
grangians in closed monotone symplectic manifolds (i.e. those for which
the first Chern class is positively proportional to the symplectic form). A
similar result for monotone symplectic manifolds with contact-type bound-
ary was proved by Ritter and Smith in [RS].
• In the setting of relative Fukaya categories (i.e. categories associated to
a symplectic manifold with a choice of symplectic hypersurface Poincar´e
dual to the symplectic form), Perutz and Sheridan proved in [PS1] that an
equivalence of a subcategory of the derived Fukaya category with the cate-
gory of coherent sheaves on a maximally degenerate mirror family implies
that the generation criterion holds.
• Ganatra proved in [Ga2], that whenever a subcategory of the Fukaya cat-
egory is cohomologically smooth in the sense of Kontsevich (i.e. admits a
split-resolution of the diagonal bi-module), the open-closed map is neces-
sarily injective. In particular, the hypothesis of Theorem 1.8 can be verified
by comparing ranks. We postpone the discussion of how Ganatra’s results
apply in our setting to Remark 23.6.


## Page 12


12
M. ABOUZAID, K. FUKAYA, Y.-G. OH, H. OHTA, K. ONO
• See [AT, Ga3, GPS, OS, PS2, Sd, Sh2, Sh3] and etc. for recent references
related to open-closed Gromov-Witten theory. The papers [Ga3, GPS, PS2]
etc. discuss exact Lagrangians in an exact symplectic manifold, and then
study Floer theory of a compact symplectic manifold X with a divisor D,
which is Poincar´e dual to the symplectic form, as a deformation of the
Floer theory on X \D. Such Floer theory is used in [Se4, Sh1] etc. to prove
homological Mirror symmetry in various cases. We have no doubt that the
A∞category (together with open-closed and closed-open maps) obtained
in their method will be embedded to the one we construct directly on X
in this paper, over Novikov field, and they are quasi isomorphic in various
cases. (We do not try to prove it in this paper.)
Outline of the paper. The results contained in Part 1 of this paper concern
constructions in homological algebra which are for the most part well-established
in the setting of ordinary categories, but for which no adequate reference seems
to exist in the presence of curvature. The main purpose of Section 3 is to recall
the notion of a curved cyclic A∞category, and of a weakly curvature free object
in such a category. In Sections 4 and 6 we define the Hochschild homology and
cohomology of such categories with coefficients in an A∞bi-module, and prove
that the cyclic structure defines a duality between these groups. In Section 5 we
define the category of twisted complexes, which will be our model for the bounded
derived category of an A∞category, and discuss the notion of split-generation of
an A∞category. Finally, Section 6 studies the problem of constructing a cyclic A∞
category from a homotopy inverse system: we solve the problem under the further
assumption that the maps in the inverse system arise from pseudo-isotopies (see
Subsection 6.3).
In Part 2, we study Lagrangian Floer theory in a compact symplectic mani-
fold.12 Section 9 (together with Sections 10-14) provides an essentially complete
construction of the A∞structure on the Lagrangian Floer cohomology of a finite
collection of Lagrangian submanifolds; this includes in particular the construction
of pseudo-isotopies which is implemented in Section 9.5. In Section 10 we discuss
the construction of homotopy unit in our de Rham setting. (The reason we need
to work with homotopy unit (but not with strict unit) is explained in Section 8.)
In Section 11 we discuss how to use bounding cochain to eliminate the curvature.
In Section 12 we reduce the A∞structure on a chain model (the de Rham complex
in our case) to its homology. The new point in this paper is to perform this con-
struction in the situation when the inner product (with cyclic symmetry) and the
homotopy unit are given.
The results of Sections 9-12 rely on the constructions of Kuranishi structures
on the moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic discs with Lagrangian boundary con-
ditions, and of CF-perturbations.
These constructions are given in Sections 13
and 14, after reviewing the necessary notions. Among the new results which we
12Some of the results of Section 9-19 are generalisations, to the case of multiple Lagrangians,
of results which have been established, in [FOOO1, FOOO4, FOOO6] either in the case of a
single Lagrangian, or in the special situation of toric manifolds. There are several other results
which prove these results under additional assumption such as exactness or monotonicity. In the
generality we work in this paper, those results are new.


## Page 13


QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY AND SPLIT GENERATION
13
introduce in these sections is a method of obtaining Kuranishi structures and CF-
perturbations in a way compatible with forgetful maps. They are essential for the
A∞structure we obtain to be unital.
In Part 3 we study the relationship between the Lagrangian Floer theory to
quantum cohomology.
In Section 15, we define the map ˆqb from the quantum
cohomology of the ambient space to the Hochschild cohomology of the A∞category
constructed in the previous part. In Section 16 we define a map ˆpb from Hochschild
homology13 to the homology of the ambient space. In Section 17 we prove that the
map ˆpb is dual to ˆqb. In Section 18 we show that those maps are non-trivial under
the assumption that the Floer homology is non-trivial. In Section 19, we prove
that ˆqb is a ring homomorphism with respect to the natural structures on either
side. In Section 20 we begin implementing the homological interpretation of the
annulus argument (see [Ab]) to the setting of this paper. Section 21 is devoted to
the discussion on orientation and sign. The novel point in this section is the part
related to the Cardy relation.
In Part 4 of the paper, we provide applications of the algebraic structures intro-
duced in Part 1 to the study of Lagrangian Floer theory. Sections 23, 24, 25, 26,
27 and 28 prove Theorems 1.7, 1.8 1.14, 1.16, 1.17 and 1.20, respectively; these are
the main results of the paper. They show that, under the assumption of Theorem
1.7, the A∞category and the quantum cohomology decompose as a direct sum
of the potential values appearing non-trivially in the decomposition L = ∪λLλ.
The next three sections concern this decomposition in the absence of this strong
assumption: In Section 29 we show that the images of the Hochschild homologies
of the categories Lλ under ˆpb are orthogonal with respect to quantum multiplica-
tion. In Section 30, we compare the decomposition of quantum cohomology into
indecomposable factors with the decomposition of the category L by potential val-
ues, while in Section 31 we prove, under a Calabi-Yau assumption, a conjecture of
Kontsevich about the relation of these decompositions with the eigenvalues of the
quantum product with the first Chern class. The paper ends with Section 32, in
which we state a quantitative version of the main results, that allows us to provide
bounds from below on the minimal areas of holomorphic disks with boundary on
Lagrangian submanifolds.
The authors thank to L. Amorim who pointed out (in 2019 February) an im-
portant issue related to the unitality and cyclic symmetry in the case of immersed
Lagrangian or several mutually transversal Lagrangians. (Such an issue does not
exist when studying disks bounding a single embedded Lagrangian.14) (See Sec-
tion 8.) They also thank Kyoji Saito and Atsushi Takahashi for their discussions
regarding the topics studied in Sections 26, 27. K. Fukaya wants to thank Aliakbar
Daemi for discussions especially those related to Subsection 13.2.

Part 1. Homological algebra of A∞categories.
3. Curved A∞categories.
This is the first of three sections studying the homological algebra of curved A∞
categories. While most of the results we describe are well-known in the absence of
curvature, there seems to be no account in the literature that proves precisely what
we want.
We shall consider a strictly unital and weakly curvature free A∞category C
defined over a commutative ring R (see Definition 3.3). In our applications, this
ring will be either the Novikov field Λ, the Novikov ring Λ0, or a quotient of the
Novikov ring by a closed ideal. Note that, to construct categories over Λ0, we shall
need the more specialized notion of a gapped filtered category, which we discuss in
Section 6.

slightly from the standard setting, we start by describing our framework.

3.2. Matrix factorisations in dimension 0. In this section, we consider the
category of matrix factorisations, which plays the same rˆole, in the study of weakly
curvature free A∞categories, as the category of complexes does in the study of
ordinary categories.
Recall that a matrix factorisation V over a ring R consists of a Z2 graded free
R-modules V , and a degree 1 endomorphism ∂V which squares to a multiple of the
identity by a scalar PO(V ). We write V as the direct sum
V 0 ⊕V 1[1]
(3.17)
and ∂i
V for the restriction of ∂V to V i[i].
18We say ‘unital’ in place of ‘strictly unital’ sometimes in this paper.

M. ABOUZAID, K. FUKAYA, Y.-G. OH, H. OHTA, K. ONO
Remark 3.5. In the algebraic literature, the condition of vanishing Euler charac-
teristic is sometimes imposed on the category of matrix factorisations. For non-
trivial values of the potential, the condition that the differential square to a non-
trivial multiple of the identity implies that the ranks of V 0 and V 1 agree, hence the
Euler characteristic vanishes. For the trivial value, we shall not impose this, because
Floer cochain complexes CF((L1, b1), (L0, b0)) with PO(L0, b0) = PO(L1, b1) need
not satisfy this property.
The collection of matrix factorisations forms a category [Ei, Or], in fact a curved
differential graded category in which every object has curvature PO(V ) · idV , and
with vanishing higher multiplications. The morphism spaces are the graded vector
spaces Mat(V, W) = HomR(V, W), which are equipped with an endomorphism
dMatϕ = ∂W ◦ϕ −(−1)deg(ϕ)ϕ ◦∂V
(3.18)
and the composition is given by the usual composition of linear maps. Note that
we are using the standard conventions from differential graded algebra, so that
the integer deg(ϕ) appearing in the above equation is the ordinary (not shifted)
grading.
The tensor product of matrix factorisations equips Mat with a monoidal struc-
ture: V ⊗W is defined to be
 (V 0 ⊗W 0) ⊕(V 1 ⊗W 1) ⊕ (V 1 ⊗W 0) ⊕(V 0 ⊗W 1) [1]
(3.19)
with differential
∂V ⊗W (v ⊗w) = ∂V (v) ⊗w + (−1)deg(v)v ⊗∂W (w)
(3.20)
which has curvature PO(V ) + PO(W). In particular, we obtain an honest differ-
ential whenever the sum vanishes:
Lemma 3.6. If PO(V ) = r and PO(W) = −r, then
r · H∗(V ⊗W, ∂V ⊗W ) = 0.
(3.21)
Proof. Let ∂0
V denote the component of ∂V mapping V 0 to V 1. The reader may
directly compute that the endomorphism of V ⊗W which vanishes on V 1 ⊗W and
is given by ∂0
V ⊗idW on V 0 ⊗W is a null-homotopy for r · idV ⊗W .
□
For each scalar r ∈R, we obtain a curvature-free subcategory Matr.
Lemma 3.7. The cohomology groups of all morphism spaces Matr are annihilated
by r, i.e.:
r · H∗ HomR(W, V ), dMat = 0
(3.22)
whenever PO(W) = PO(V ) = r.
Proof. It suffices to prove that the identity in H∗ HomR(V, V ), dMat is annihilated
by r. Consider the map
∂0

the class represented by idV in H∗ HomR(V, V ), dMat vanishes when multiplied by

isomorphism of chain complexes for all pairs (X, Y ) of objects with the
same potential value.
(2) An A∞isomorphism is a linear A∞quasi-isomorphism.

3.4. Cyclic structure. The A∞-categories we consider have an additional struc-
ture which comes from Poincar´e duality on the cohomology of a compact manifold:
Definition 3.13. A cyclic pairing of dimension n on A∞category C is a collection
of linear maps
⟨· , ·⟩: C(X, Y ) ⊗C(Y, X) →R

cohomology with respect to mC

An A∞category C is called cyclic if it is equipped with a non-degenerate cyclic
pairing.

4. Hochschild (co)-homology.
Most of our focus will be on Hochschild homology and cohomology as invariants
of A∞categories which depend only on their quasi-isomorphism type, and which are
respectively obtained by deriving the tensor product with the diagonal bi-module,
and its space of morphisms.
Since the setting is not exactly standard outside of symplectic topology, we shall
give a complete definition of the Hochschild homology and cohomology for a curved
A∞category C in which all objects are weakly unobstructed. We then prove the
following two results, which are well known in slightly different contexts:
Proposition 4.1. The Hochschild cohomology of C is an algebra with respect to
the cup product, over which Hochschild homology admits a natural module structure
via the cap product.
Proof. The algebra structure is established in Lemma 4.22, and the module struc-
ture in Lemma 4.23.
□
Next, we assume that C admits a pairing of dimension n (see Definition 3.13),
and prove the following result (see Lemma 4.20 for a precise formula).
Proposition 4.2. The pairing on C induces a pairing
HH∗(C, C) ⊗HH∗−n(C, C) →Λ.
(4.1)
If C is cyclic, this pairing is perfect.
□
4.1. A∞bi-modules. The proper setting for defining Hochschild (co)-homology
are categories of bi-modules. Given curved A∞categories A and B, an object of
the category of A-B-bi-modules consists of R modules P(X, Y ) for each pair of
objects X ∈Ob(A) and Y ∈Ob(B), together with structure maps mP
r|1|s defined
whenever r and s are non-negative integers, which generalize the notion of left and
right multiplication. Our conventions for signs agree with those in [Se3].
Remark 4.3. The reader may notice that all bi-modules we shall consider will
have vanishing curvature (this is implicit in the definition as the curvature would
correspond to m0|0|0). There is a notion of curved bi-module, which has a rˆole to
play in symplectic topology, but with whose investigation we shall not be concerned.

5. Triangulated closure and split-generation.
Given a closed morphism f : X →Y in an A∞category C, the notion of a cone
Cone(f) is well defined up to quasi-isomorphism: this is an object of C, equipped
with morphisms Cone(f) →X and Y →Cone(f), which induce an exact sequence
on morphism spaces.
While the notion is well-defined, such an object may not necessarily exist; an
A∞category is triangulated if all closed morphisms admit cones. Moreover, ev-
ery category admits a quasi-embedding into a triangulated category. In a sense,
the most canonical such choice is the Yoneda embedding into the category of left
modules.


## Page 34


34
M. ABOUZAID, K. FUKAYA, Y.-G. OH, H. OHTA, K. ONO
The main disadvantage of the Yoneda embedding is that it is only a quasi-
embedding, making computations sometimes cumbersome; i.e. the morphism spaces
between Yoneda modules do not in general have finite rank even if all morphism
spaces in C have finite rank. It is partly for this reason that we prefer to work with
twisted complexes, following [Se2].
5.1. Twisted complexes. In this subsection, we define an enlargement of C which
we call the category of curved twisted complexes Tw(C) over C, and which serves
as a model for the derived category of C.
The category of twisted complexes is enriched over the category of matrix fac-
torisations, and an object X• ⊠V• of such a category consists of the following
data:

require that Vi = 0 for all but finitely many i.

5.4. Idempotent closures. Let C be an A∞category, and X a weakly unob-
structed object:
Definition 5.8. A cohomological idempotent ι ∈H∗(C)(X, X) is a morphism sat-
isfying
ι · ι = ι.
(5.35)
Whenever Y is a summand of X, the composition X →Y →X is an idempotent.
In an idempotent closed category, every idempotent arises from a summand:
Definition 5.9. C is idempotent closed if every idempotent is represented by an
object, i.e. if for every morphism ι satisfying Equation (5.35), there exists an object
Y , and morphisms
[f] ⊗[g] ∈H∗(C)(X, Y ) ⊗H∗(C)(Y, X)
(5.36)
such that
[f] · [g] = ι
(5.37)
[g] · [f] = eY .

We say that D is an idempotent closure of C if (i) there is a quasi-embedding
Tw(C) →D, such that every object of D is quasi-isomorphic to a summand of
an object in the image of Φ and (ii) D is idempotent closed. The existence and
uniqueness of idempotent closures up to quasi-isomorphism is a standard fact in the
theory of triangulated categories; the A∞analogue was proved by Seidel in [Se2,
Lemma 4.7 and Remark 4.10] which we summarize:
Lemma 5.10. Every A∞category C has an idempotent closure Twπ(C) which ad-
mits an embedding Tw(C) →Twπ(C), and which is quasi-equivalent to any idem-
potent closure of C.
□
Observe that, whenever B ⊂C is an A∞embedding, we have a commuting
diagram of A∞embeddings:

Definition 5.11. An object Y of C, lies in the category split-generated by B if its
image in Tw(C), is a summand of an object in Tw(B). If this property is satisfied
by every object, we say that B split-generates C.
Lemma 5.12. If a subcategory B ⊂C split-generates, the inclusion Twπ(B) ⊂
Twπ(C) is an A∞equivalence.
Proof. Consider the smallest subcategory of Twπ(C) which contains B, and is closed
under quasi-isomorphisms, cones, and summands. By assumption, this subcategory
includes every object of C, hence must agree with Twπ(C). The result then follows
from [Se2, Corollary 4.9].
□
6. Gapped filtered A∞categories.
The purpose of this section is to discuss a special class of A∞categories over
Λ0 which we call gapped filtered following [FOOO1], and which are required for the
inductive constructions of the categories in Section 9.
In Section 9 there is a situation where we need to study filtered A∞categories
which are not unital. So in Section 6 and Parts 2 and 3 we do not assume a filtered
A∞category is unital unless we say it is unital explicitly. In Sections 3-5 and Part
4, all A∞categories are assumed to be (strictly) unital unless otherwise mentioned
explicitly.
6.1. Discrete monoids and gapped morphisms. A discrete submonoid G of
R≥0 is a subset of R≥0 such that
(1) 0 ∈G,
(2) if g1, g2 ∈G then g1 + g2 ∈G,
(3) G is a discrete subset.
Note that the last condition implies that {g ∈G | g ≤E} is a finite set for any E.
We denote by ΛG the subring of Λ0 consisting of the formal sums P aiT λi with
λi ∈G.
We say discrete monoid instead of discrete submonoid of R≥0 for simplicity.

6.3. Pseudoisotopy of A∞category. The notion of pseudo-isotopy of cyclic
filtered A∞algebra was introduced in [Fu2, Definition 8.7]. We can generalize the
notion to the cyclic filtered A∞category as follows.
Let C be a cyclic filtered A∞category, which we assume is G-gapped.24 We
consider one of the following situations. In an algebraic situation we assume that for
each pair of objects X, Y the morphism space C(X, Y ) is a finitely generated free Λ0
module. In the geometric situation we includethe case when some morphism spaces
are given by the space of differential forms on a Lagrangian. More precisely, in
Section 9, we shall construct a category whose objects X = (L, θL) are Lagrangian
submanifolds equipped with extra data. Given a pair X = (L, θL) and Y = (L, θL)
whose underlying Lagrangians agree, we shall have
C(X, Y ) = Ω(L)“⊗Λ0.

module the maximal ideal, i.e.

submanifolds do not agree, we shall assume that L is transversal to L′. In this case,
C(X, Y ) is a finitely generated free Λ0 module, as in the algebraic situation.

We say that two A∞categories are strongly isomorphic if there exists a linear
A∞functor between them that induces a bijection on objects and isomorphisms on
morphism spaces. We can define the notion of strong isomorphism of pseudo-isotopy
in the same way.
Remark 6.9. In [FOOO1] the isomorphism between two A∞algebras is defined
to be an A∞homomorphism that has an inverse.
The above notion of strong
isomorphism requires much stronger conditions than that. In fact we assume that
it is linear. Namely it is {Φk} such that Φk = 0 for k ̸= 1.
We can prove that pseudo-isotopy is an equivalence relation in the same way as
[Fu2, Lemma 8.2].

Remark 6.12. For the cyclic filtered A∞algebra, a similar result was proved in
[Fu2, Theorem 8.2]. There the morphism ΨC is constructed explicitly by summation
over trees. The same proof as that of [Fu2, Theorem 8.2] can be also applied to our
case, which in fact will produce the same functor as the one we define here.
Remark 6.13. Existence of the homotopy equivalence C →D for which Diagram
(6.34) commutes is an immediate consequence of the fact that Evalt=0 : C →C has
a homotopy inverse. (See [Fu1, Proposition 8.9].) However there does not seem to
be an appropriate inner product on C to make it a cyclic A∞category. So to obtain
a cyclic functor we need to work a bit more. This is a reason why we introduced
the notion of cyclic pseudo-isotopy.

(1) CE′ is strongly isomorphic to the reduction of ˜CE modulo T E′.

is strongly isomorphic to the reduction of ˜CE.
The unital version also holds.

6.4. Homotopy inverse limits.
Definition 6.15. A homotopy inverse system of G-gapped cyclic filtered A∞cat-
egories consists of the following data:
(1) an increasing sequence of non-negative real numbers Ei for which limi Ei =
+∞,
(2) a G-gapped cyclic filtered A∞category CEi defined over Λ0/T Ei, and
(3) a G-gapped quasi-isomorphism between CEi and the reduction of CEi+1
modulo T Ei, which is cyclic.
Note that we do not assume filtered A∞category to be unital here. So the notion
of quasi equivalence is not defined. In fact we need unit to define the notion that two
objects are quasi equivalent. On the other hand, the notion of quasi isomorphism
is defined without using unit, as follows:


## Page 50


50
M. ABOUZAID, K. FUKAYA, Y.-G. OH, H. OHTA, K. ONO
Definition 6.16. Let C, D be non-unital curved filtered A∞categores. A filtered
A∞functor Φ : C →D is said to be a quasi-isomorphism if it is bijection on objects
and if Φ : C(X, Y )/Λ+ →D(X, Y )/Λ+ induces a chain homotopy equivalence on
the chain complex with boundary operator m1.
In the same way as the case of filtered A∞algebra (proved in [FOOO1, Theorem
4.2.45]), we can show that it implies that Φ has homotopy inverse.
We say that a gapped cyclic A∞category C
←−defined over Λ0 is a homotopy
inverse limit of a given homotopy inverse system, if the reduction of C
←−modulo T Ei
is gapped quasi-isomorphic to CEi, which is cyclic, for each i such that the following
diagram strictly commutes as functors filtered A∞categories over Λ0/T Ei:
C

It is reasonable to expect that every inverse system has a homotopy inverse
limit, but in the context we describe (of gapped cyclic filtered A∞categories), such
a result has not been proved. (In case we do not consider cyclic symmetry, the
existence of a homotopy inverse limit is proved in [FOOO1, Subsection 7.2.8]).
However if the homotopy equivalences are induced by pseudo-isotopies, the next
result asserts the existence of homotopy inverse limit (the case of cyclic and unital
filtered A∞algebras was proved in [Fu2, Section 12]):
Proposition 6.17. An inverse system CEi of gapped cyclic filtered A∞categories
has a homotopy inverse limit, which is cyclic, if the quasi-isomorphism between
CEi and the reduction of CEi+1 modulo T Ei is induced by a cyclic pseudo-isotopy
for each i, in the sense of Proposition 6.11.
The unital version also holds.

between CEi and the reduction modulo T Ei of CEi+1. We will prove the following

(3) CEi,i = CEi and CEi,i = CEi.

to CEi,j′−1 and CEi,j′−1 respectively.

6.5. Transfer of bounding cochains. In the context of the previous section, we
would like to describe a choice of bounding cochains on an object of the homotopy
inverse limit of an inverse system in terms of bounding cochains on the underlying
categories of the inverse system. The starting point is to be able to relate bounding
cochains on pseudo-isotopic A∞algebras.

filtered A∞categories, respectively defined modulo T E and T E′. Let C be a pseudo-
isotopy modulo T E between C and the reduction modulo T E of D.

Part 2. A∞category in Lagrangian Floer theory.
7. Filtered A∞algebra on de Rham complex associated to a single
Lagrangian submanifold.
This section is a review of the construction of [FOOO1]. We rewrite it using de
Rham cohomology and also include some enhancements.

Remark 7.4. There is one technical point to take care of in the statements, such as
Proposition 7.3, appearing many times in this paper. Namely to prove Proposition
7.3 as stated above, we need to study infinitely many moduli spaces and construct
Kuranishi strutures or CF-perturbations etc. on them which are all compatible.
The construction of such objects is done by induction on the energy ω(β) of ho-
mology classes β. We then meet a trouble which is described in detail in [FOOO1,
Subsection 7.2.3]. In [FOOO1, Subsection 7.2] a method to go around this trouble
is given. Namely we prove such a statement only up to an arbitrary but fixed en-
ergy level E for each E and then we use the technique of homological algebra to
promote the structure to the higher energy level. This method is applicable to the
situation of this paper.
We actually do it in the following way:
(1) We construct Kuranishi structures and CF-perturbations for the moduli
spaces of holomorphic maps with energy ≤E for each but fixed E ≥0.
(2) We use these data to define operations or maps up to energy level E.
(3) Then the equalities hold only modulo T E.
(4) We take a sequence Ei of positive numbers that diverges to infinity and
construct a filtered A∞category CEi over Λ0/T Ei for each i.
(5) For Ei < Ej we prove that CEi is homotopy equivalent to CEj over Λ0/T Ei.
(We moreover prove that they are pseudo-isotopic in the sense of Definition
6.7.)
(6) We use homological algebra to take the inverse limit of CEi and obtain a
filtered A∞category over Λ0.
The mod T E version has some additional applications which we discuss in Section
32.
We implement this strategy in detail in Subsections 9.4 and 9.5 for the construc-
tion of the category in Section 9. Meanwhile for simplicity we state Proposition 7.3
as above, leaving implicit the fact that the following line should be added before
the statement:
“For each E ≥0 the following holds for all β with ω(β) ≤E.”
Many of the equalities appearing in this paper are actually hold mod T E only
during Steps (1)-(3) above.
Remark 7.5. Actually the Kuranishi structures and CF-perturbations we use are
not compatible with the forgetful map of the interior marked points. See Remark
9.22 for its reason. So we actually need a double induction on the energy E and
the number ℓof interior marked points.
The notion of CF-perturbations31 is introduced to make sense of the integration
along fibers in the realm of Kuranishi structures (see [FOOO15, Chapter 7]). We
follow the way of [FOOO15] to construct a system of CF-perturbations on the outer
collaring Mℓ;k+1(L; β)⊞1 of Mℓ;k+1(L; β). (See Definition 13.7 for the definition of
the outer collaring.)
31In [FOOO4], a slightly different version, “continuous families of perturbations”, is used to
integrate along the fibers.

Proposition 7.6. There exists a system of CF-perturbations on the moduli spaces
Mℓ;k+1(L; β)⊞1 such that the following holds:
(1) It is transversal to zero.
(2) It is compatible with the description of the boundary in Proposition 7.3 (5)
above.
(3) It is compatible with the forgetful map of the boundary marked points.32
(4) It is compatible with the permutation of interior marked points.
(5) It is compatible with cyclic permutation of the boundary marked points.
(6) The evaluation map ev0 is strongly submersive with respect to this CF-
perturbation.33
The CF-perturbations satisfying (1), (2), (4), (6) above is constructed in [FOOO15,
Chapter 22] (and Subsection 14.2), while the system of CF-perturbations satisfying
(3)(5) in addition is constructed in [Fu2, Corollary 5.1]. See Subsection 14.3.
Remark 7.7.
(1) In [FOOO15], to carry out the inductive construction of a system of CF-
pertrubations in [FOOO15, Chapter 22] we used the consistency of Ku-
ranishi structures and CF-perturbations not only along the boundary but
also along the corners. In other words we used the corner compatibility
conditions. However we do not need the corner compatibility conditions in
this paper by the reason explained in Remark 14.7.
(2) There is an issue of smoothness of the forgetful map on the outer collar.
We will discuss it in Section 14.

8. Cyclic symmetry and unitality.
We start with a bit technical discussion about the construction of (abstract)
perturbation to obtain a cyclically symmetric, unital, and filtered A∞category. We
use the moduli spaces Mℓ;k+1(L; β) of pseudo-holomorphic disks with a boundary
condition given by a single (embedded) Lagrangian submanifold L. We also use
the moduli space of pseudo-holomorphic polygons Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B) (see Section 9
for this notation.) with the boundary condition given by a finite set of Lagrangian
submanifolds. The former gives an A∞algebra and the latter an A∞category. We
want to use some of the properties mentioned in Section 6.

1 on Lκi. (Lκi = Lκi+1). The first formula is the cyclic symmetry (3.30), the second
is the unitality. For the cyclic symmetry we need to make the perturbation we use to
be symmetric under the cyclic permutation of the boundary marked points. For the
unitality, we need the perturbation to be compatible with respect to the forgetful
map of the boundary marked points. In the case of a single embedded Lagrangian
submanifold we can find such a perturbation as we discussed in Section 7. However
in the case of several (mutually transversal) Lagrangian submanifolds (or of a single
immersed Lagrangian submanifold) it is actually impossible to find a perturbation
so that both of the properties are satisfied, in case we use de Rham model.34
In fact the following explicit example illustrates such a situation.35 Let L0 and
L1 be two embedded Lagrangian submanifolds which intersect transversally at one
point p. We consider the moduli space of pseudo-holomorphic maps u : D2 →X

In fact we consider the configuration of one strip [0, 1] × R →X between L0
and L1 with the ends being asymptotic to q and p, respectively.
We consider
one marked points w0 ∈{0} × R. We want to find a perturbation so that it is
compatible with the forgetful map at w0. It can be induced from the translation
invariant perturbation of the strip (without marked point). We however consider
the limit when w0 goes to +∞. Then the limit in the stable map topology consists
of two components, one is a strip without marked point, the other is a constant
map to p with 3 marked points 2 of which are mapped to p01, p10, and the third
is mapped to somewhere on L0. (See Figure 2.) This is exactly the configuration
discussed above. Therefore if we introduce non-trivial perturbation for this second

A way to resolve this issue is to replace various symmetries by homotopy (or
infinite homotopy) symmetries. In this paper we take the route of replacing the uni-
tality by the homotopy unitality but keeping cyclic symmetry to be exact. (There
is a homotopy version of cyclic symmetry, which is called Calabi-Yau property. We
do not use it in this paper.) Strict unitality will be restored when we go to the
canonical model in Section 12.
Remark 8.1. In [Fu3] a strictly unital filtered A∞category is constructed from a
finite set of relatively spin Lagrangian submanifolds in a similar situation. In [Fu3]
a strictly unital filtered A∞category without cyclic symmetry is constructed.

We fix a bulk class b ∈H∗(X; ΛC
0 ) in this section. We start from a finite collection

Condition 9.1.

Theorem 9.3. There exists a curved cyclic gapped filtered A∞category Lform
curve
over Λ0 whose set of objects is L , with morphism spaces from Li to Lj given by

Remark 9.4. We remark that the A∞category in Theorem 9.3 is not unital. The
reason was explained in Section 8. We need unitality for applications of our main
theorems, since in many important cases such as the toric case, we need to study
weakly unobstructed Lagrangian submanifolds rather than unobstructed ones. For
this purpose we need unit.
We recall:
Definition 9.5. A homotopically unital curved filtered A∞category is a pair of
curved filtered A∞category C and a strictly unital curved filtered A∞category C+
together with a linear filtered A∞functor ι : C →C+ with the following properties.
(1) ι is a bijection on objects.
(2) If c ̸= c′, c, c′ ∈Ob(C) then C+(c, c′) = C(c, c′).

Proposition 9.18.
(1) Each of the spaces
◦
Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B) has a compactification Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B)
that is Hausdorff and metrizable.
(2) The space Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B)⊞1 has a Kuranishi structure.
(3) The boundary of Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B)⊞1 is a union of the three types of fiber or
direct products (9.9), (9.10), (9.11). Here the union is taken over the data
described above together with the shuffles (L′′, L′) of ℓsuch that #L′′ = ℓ′′,
#L′ = ℓ′.
The Kuranishi structure on the first factor of (9.9) is the one in Propo-
sition 7.3.
The orientations are compatible in the same sense as Proposition 7.3 (3).
(4) The evaluation maps (9.8) extend to the compactification and are compatible
with the description of the boundary in (3).
(5) For each i ∈K
: and j ∈{1, . . . , ki}, the evaluation map
evi,j : Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B)⊞1 →˜Lκi
is weakly submersive.
(6) The Kuranishi structure is invariant under permutation of the interior
marked points.
(7) The Kuranishi structure is invariant under cyclic permutation of the bound-
ary marked points in the sense we describe below.

Remark 9.22. We explain the reason why we do not claim our Kuranishi structures
and CF-perturbations are compatible with the forgetful map of the interior marked
points.41 (The reason is different from the reason why CF-perturbations are not

compatible with the forgetful map of the boundary marked points. The latter is

We consider an element p = (Σ; u;⃗z+,⃗z; ⃗⃗w) of Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B). To define its
Kuranishi neighborhood we consider u′ close to u and study equation
∂u′ ∈Ep(x).
(9.16)
Here x is u′ together with Σ′,⃗z′+,⃗z′, ⃗⃗w′. The linear space Ep(x) is an obstruction
space that is a finite dimensional subspace of Γ(Σ′; (u′)∗TX ⊗Λ01). We require that
the support of elements Ep(x) are away from interior nodes. The reason why we
do so is that, then, we can use the trivialization of the universal family of Deligne-
Mumford moduli space to identify δ-thick parts Σ′(δ) and Σ(δ) of Σ′ and of Σ.42
To require this property we also require that the support of an element of Ep(x)
is away from interior marked points. In fact when we consider the case where two
interior marked points z+
i and z+
j become the same point in the limit, there will be
a new sphere bubble appearing at that point. If the support of an element of Ep(x)
contains z+
i
and z+
j then the support of an element of the obstruction space (=
fiber of the obstruction bundle) of the limit necessarily contains the interior nodes.
Now it is easy to observe that there is no reasonable way to require simultane-
ously that the support of an element of Ep(x) is away from interior marked points
and that it is compatible with the forgetful map of the interior marked points.
Note that we always require that the the support of an element of Ep(x) is away
from boundary. So in particular it is away from the boundary nodes or boundary
marked points.
So the above mentioned reason does not apply to the forgetful
map of boundary marked points. In fact in Proposition 7.3 we did claim that the
Kuranishi structure is compatible with the forgetful map of the boundary marked
points.
9.2. Construction of the cyclic A∞category 2 : Orientation. In this sub-
section, we describe the data which determine orientation of the moduli space
Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B) introduced in the last subsection. We also describe the compatibil-
ity of the orientations with the identification mentioned in Proposition 9.18 (3) of
the boundary. The discussion here follows [FOOO9].
We recall that we fix a background class st ∈H2(X; Z2) and assume our La-
grangian submanifolds are st-relatively spin. More precisely we need to fix a rela-
tively spin structure. Let us review its definition now. We fix a smooth triangulation
of X which includes each Lagrangian submanifold we study as a simplicial subcom-
plex. We take an oriented real vector bundle V (st) on the 3-skeleton X(3) of X
whose second Stiefel-Whiteney class is st.
Definition 9.23. An st-relative spin structure on a Lagrangian submanifold L is
a trivialization of TL ⊕V (st) on the 2-skeleton L(2) of L.
42Here δ-thick part Σ(δ) is by definition a connected component of {x ∈Σ | iΣ(x) > δ}. (See
[Hu].) The notation iΣ in the above formula is defined as follows. We take the double bΣ of Σ. We
remove boundary marked points and the doubles of interior marked points from bΣ to obtain bΣ0.
When Σ together with marked points are stable the irreducible components of bΣ0 have unique
metrics of constant negative curvature −1. The notation iΣ stands for its injectivity radious. We
say a complement of Σ(δ) a δ-thin part. In case Σ together with marked points is unstable we
add additional marked points using u.


## Page 78


78
M. ABOUZAID, K. FUKAYA, Y.-G. OH, H. OHTA, K. ONO
If L is st-relative spin, allowing the trivialization of TL⊕V (st) on the 2-skeleton
L(2) to vary, the set of all st-relative structures forms a principal homogeneous space
of the group H2(X, L; Z2) and this set is independent of the choice of triangulation.
(See [FOOO1, Proposition 8.1.6].)
In this paper, we choose a real vector bundle V (st) and fix it. The set of spin
structures on TL ⊕V (st) is a principal homogeneous space of H1(L; Z2).
The
notion of stable conjugacy among them is defined in [FOOO2, Definition 8.1.5]
with V1 = V2 = V (st). Then the set of stable conjugacy classes of spin structures
on TL ⊕V (st) is a principal homogeneous space of H1(L; Z2)/(Im(H1(X; Z2) →
H1(L; Z2))), which is a subgroup of H2(X, L; Z2). Note also that the orientation
on the moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic disks only depends on the stable con-
jugacy class of relative spin structures, see [FOOO2, Proposition 8.1.16].
In this paper we consider various finite sets L of st-relative spin Lagrangian sub-
manifolds. We always choose and fix an st-relative spin structure on each element
L of L .
We next take (L, θ), (L′, θ′) ∈L , which are transversal to each other. We take
a path λp : [0, 1] →LAG+(Tp(X)) for each p ∈L ∩L′ such that
λp(0) = TpL,
λp(1) = TpL′.
(9.17)
Here LAG+(Tp(X)) is the oriented Lagrangian Grassmannian, that is the set of
oriented n dimensional subspaces of TpX on which the symplectic form ω vanishes.
We make a similar choice for self-intersection points of immersed Lagrangians L.
Remark 9.24. In case the symplectic manifold X satisfies c1(X) = 0, and we have
chosen a complex volume form on X, we may restrict attention to Lagrangians
equipped with a trivialisation of the phase map to S1 (i.e. graded Lagrangians).
Assuming that all (L, θL) ∈L are graded Lagrangians, this data determines a
unique homotopy class of paths λp, associated to each transverse intersection p.

This is [FOOO9, Theorem 6.7]. We can prove a similar statement on the orien-
tation of the other moduli spaces which appear in this paper, in the same way.
We use this orientation throughout the paper. We remark that if we change op to
the opposite orientation, then the orientation of all the moduli spaces which contain
p are affected. This is actually equivalent to replacing the generator [p] ∈CF(L, L′)

those choices ˜λp, op in our notation for filtered A∞category.
In this paper, we construct filtered A∞-structures on the de Rham complexes
with Λ0 coefficients as in [FOOO3], [FOOO4], [FOOO6], [FOOO11] while we did so
on certain subcomplexes of singular chain complexes in [FOOO1], [FOOO9]. The
signs in operations m, q, p arising from the correspondence are obtained in [FOOO1]
as we explained in the last paragraph of [FOOO4].

natural number m we obtain a Z/2m grading in a similar way.

Below we explain a case where the perturbation of the constant map is involved.
Example 9.35. Let us consider the case when we have two Lagrangian subman-
ifolds L0 and L1. Let p ∈L0 ∩L1. We write p01 (resp. p10) when we regard it
as an intersection point L0 ∩L1 (resp. L1 ∩L0). We consider qform
ℓ;⃗κ,⃗p;B with ℓ= 0,
⃗κ = (0, 1), B = 0 and study
qform
0;(0,1),(p01,p10);0([p01], [p10]).
(9.35)
It will be a differential form on L0 and easy degree counting shows that it is a
differential n-form where n = dim L0.
The moduli space we use to define this
operation consists of pseudo-holomorphic triangle (of area 0), so that the three
edges (in counter clockwise order) are mapped to L0, L1, L0. The 0-th marked
point is a vertex between the last and the first edges. We require first and second
marked points are mapped to p01, p10 respectively. Such a moduli space consists of
one point which is a constant map to p = p01 = p10. This space itself is transversal
but does not satisfies Proposition 9.18 (5). Namely the evaluation map at the 0-th
marked point is not a submersion to L0. If we use this (unperturbed) moduli space
then (9.35) would be a delta form supported at p ∈L0. Since we are constructing

need to include an obstruction bundle and an appropriate CF-perturbation. Then
(9.35) becomes an n-form which is a smoothing of the delta form. The n-form itself
depends on the CF-perturbation.
We remark that here we need to perturb the moduli space of constant maps.
The compatibility with forgetful map then becomes an issue. This point is further
discussed in Sections 10 and 13.

We next use the bulk class to define a curved cyclic filtered A∞category Lform

We mention that the endomorphism space of any object is given by the de Rham

For the next statement, we recall the splitting (7.11) of the ambient cohomology

10. The forgetful map and unitality.
As we mentioned several times, the filtered A∞structure constructed in Section
9 is not unital. The purpose of this section is to describe the way to obtain a filtered
homotopically unital A∞structure.
10.1. Cyclicity of homotopically unital filtered A∞category. The notion of
homotopically unital curved filtered A∞category is defined in Definition 9.5. We
define cyclicity of such category below.
Let C be a filtered A∞category and c its object. We define CHkC(c, c) as the
set of finite sums of elements of the form h = h0 ⊗· · · ⊗hk. Here c0, . . . , ck are
objects with c0 = c and hi ∈C(ci, ci+1), where ck+1 = c0 by convention.
Let
C+(c, c) = C(c, c) ⊕Λ0[e+
c ] ⊕Λ0[fc] as in Definition 9.5.
Definition 10.1. A homotopically unital filtered A∞category (C, C+, ι) is said to
be cyclic if the following holds.
(1) There is an inner product ⟨∗, ∗⟩: C(c, c′) ⊗C(c′, c) as in Definition 3.13.45
(2) There exists an operator
+mk : CHkC+(c, c) →Λ0.
such that:

10.2. Forgetable and unforgetable marked points. We use the notation of
Subsection 9.1 and consider the moduli space Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B) in Proposition 9.18.
We recall that for a category to be homotopically unital we add two additional
generators e+
c and fc for each object c. The former will be the (strict) unit and the
latter will be a ‘homotopy’ between e+
c and the original (homotopy) unit ec. We
introduce two auxiliary data fe and ff for this purpose. Detailed definitions thereof
are now in order.
We start with fe. It is a map which assigns a subset fe,i of {1, . . . , ki} to each
i = 0, . . . , K. (Recall that {1, . . . , ki} is the index set of the diagonal marked points
⃗wi = (wi,1, . . . , wi,ki).) We use marked points labeled by fe to define operations
including e+
c .
To include the degree −1 element fc in our discussion, we use another forgetabil-
ity data ff. We will write
ff = {(i, j) ∈Z≥0 × Z>0 | j ∈ff,i}.

The moduli spaces Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B; fe), Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B;⃗f) are actually the same as
Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B). We however use different Kuranishi structures and CF-perturbations
on those moduli spaces.

When we write Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p, m); B; fe) or Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p, m); B;⃗f), we require the 0-th

We then put

Definition 10.4. A diagonal marked point wi,j of an element of Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B; fe)
is said to be forgetable if j ∈fe,i. Otherwise it is said to be unforgetable. Switching
marked points zi are, by definition, unforgetable. We call fe, forgetability data.
We will pull back the 0-form 1 by the evaluation maps at the marked points
labeled by fe. We also require various data and perturbations to be compatible
with the forgetful maps of the marked points labeled by fe. Then we will have the
strict unitality. We will carry out this process precisely below.
We consider three types of the boundary (9.9), (9.10), (9.11) of Mℓ;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B).

e, f2

(tricom1) All the marked points on the first factor are forgetable and the homology

We say the irreducible component of the first factor is a trivial component if (tri-
com1) or (tricom2) happens. In the situation (tricom1) we declare the new marked
point on the second factor to be forgetable. (Note that the object of the first factor
is the one associated to a single embedded Lagrangian appearing in Section 7. So
we do not need to distinguish forgetable and unforgetable marked points for the
marked points on this factor.) In the situation (tricom2) we declare the new marked
point on the second factor to be unforgetable. We will discuss the way to handle
this trivial component of the first factor in Proposition 10.5 (3) below.

Remark 10.6. We elaborate on the cyclic symmetry of the Kuranishi structure
of the moduli space Mℓ,⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p, m); B; fe)⊞1. It is the cyclic symmetry of the per-
mutation of marked points which are unforgetable, that is, not labeled by fe. This
is the requirement in case there exists at least one unforgetable marked points. In
case all the marked points are forgetable, the space of Mℓ,⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p, m); B; fe)⊞1 is
not defined. In this case the cyclic symmetry is a consequence of compatibility of
the Kuranishi structure with the forgetful map of Mℓ,⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B; fe)⊞1 (Proposition
10.5 (6)).

11. Elimination of the curvature.
In this section, we explain how to pass from the above curved category to the
category whose objects are pairs of a Lagrangian submanifold and its bounding
cochain.
In Definition 7.14 we defined the notion of weak bounding cochain of
an A∞algebra {mk}. Because of Lemma 11.4 we state below, this structure is
mostly the same as the restriction of the one we defined in the last section. A slight
difference is that the structure in the last section contains two extra generators fLκ
and e+
Lκ. We first give a few simple algebraic lemmas to clarify this point.
Lemma 11.1. Let (C, {mk}) be a curved and gapped filtered A∞algebra which has
a strict unit ec. Then it is homotopically unital in a canonical way.
Proof. We consider C+ = C ⊕Λ0e+
c ⊕Λ0fc and define an A∞structure on C+ as
follows: When all the inputs are in C the A∞operation is the same as one of C. If
the input contains e+
c then A∞operation is determined from the fact e+
c is the unit.
If the input contains fc then A∞operation is zero except the case it is determined
from the fact e+
c is the unit and the next formula.
m1(fc) = e+
c −ec.
□
Lemma 11.2. In the situation of Lemma 11.1, C is unitally, filtered and gapped
homotopy equivalent to C+.59
Proof. We define a unital, filtered and gapped A∞homomorphism F : C+ →C as
follows. When restricted to C ⊂C+ the map F is the identity. (In particular it is
linear there.) If the input contains e+ the map F is determined by the fact that
it is unital. If the input contains f then F is zero. It is easy to check that F is
a unitally, filtered and gapped A∞homomorphism. It is also easy to see that it
induces an isomorphism on m1 cohomology. Therefore it is a homotopy equivalence
by [FOOO1, Theorem 4.2.45a].
□
Lemma 11.3. Suppose we are in the situation of Lemma 11.1. If b is a bounding
cochain of C with potential value c then b + cf is a bounding cochain of C+ with
potential value c.
The bounding cochain b is gauge equivalent to b′ if and only if b + cf is gauge
equivalent to b′ + cf.
The proof is an easy calculation. (See [FOOO1, Remark 3.6.31].) Lemma 11.2
implies that an arbitrary bounding cochain of C+ is gauge equivalent to the one of
the form b + cf.
We observe:
Lemma 11.4. The unital A∞algebra structure on Ω(Lκ)ˆ⊗Λ0 in Proposition 7.12
induces one on (Ω(Lκ)ˆ⊗Λ0)+ in Subsection 10.3 by Lemma 11.1. This unital A∞
algebra in Section 7 coincides with one obtained by restricting the A∞structure in
Subsection 10.3.
This is a consequence of Proposition 10.5 (9) and Proposition 10.7 (11).
Thus if we have a bounding cochain b of Ω(Lκ)ˆ⊗Λ0 with potential value c then
b + cf is a bounding cochain of (Ω(Lκ)ˆ⊗Λ0)+. Sometimes we omit f and write b in
stead of b + cf .
59See [FOOO1, Subsection 4.2.3] for the definition.

12. Canonical model.
In the filtered A∞category that we defined in Section 11, the morphism space
from (Lκ, bκ) to (Lκ′, bκ′) is the de Rham complex in case Lκ = Lκ′ (or its +
version when bκ = bκ′). In certain situations, we prefer to work with a filtered
A∞category whose morphism spaces are finite dimensional. We use the canonical
model, which we construct in this subsection, for this purpose. Another reason to


## Page 107


QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY AND SPLIT GENERATION
107
go to the canonical model is an issue that the inner product are not defined for e+
Lκ
or fLκ. In this subsection we follow the strategy explained in [FOOO1, Section 5.4]
and [Fu2, Section 10]. See also [KS1]. We restrict our construction to the category
of weakly unobstructed objects, even though it would be possible to work at the
level of curved categories.

We explain a geometric background of this definition. A decorated ribbon tree as
in Definition 12.1 can be used to describe a combinatorial type of a bordered stable
disk. Suppose (Σ, (z0, z1, . . . , zN)) is a tree like union of disks with double points
together with boundary marked points enumerated in counter-clock-wise order.
Let u : Σ →X be a pseudo-holomorphic map with a certain boundary condition,
described below. Its combinatorial type is described by Γ = (T , K(·), p(·), B(·)).
An interor vertex v of the T is identified with an irreducible component Σv of Σ.
An exterior vertex is identified with one of the marked points. The exterior edge
joins an exterior vertex corresponding to a marked point zi to the vertex v such
that zi ∈Σv. An interior edge e corresponds to a double point ze and joins two
vertices v, v′ such that ze ∈Σv ∩Σv′. B(v) is the homology class of the map u
restricted to Σv. The edge e is of type s if u sends ze to an intersection point of two
Lagrangian submanifolds or is a self-intersection point of an immersed Lagrangian
submanifold, that is, the point p(e). The edge e is of type d if u sends ze to one of
the Lagrangian submanifolds. Let v be one of the interior vertices and Σv be the
corresponding irreducible component (that is, a disk). Σv contains m(v)+1 double
or marked points zei(v) (i = 0, . . . , m(v)) that corresponds to the edges ei(v). We
denote them by zi ∈∂Σv = S1. They are on the boundary in the counter-clock-wise
orientation. The part of ∂Σv between zi, zi+1 is denoted by zizi+1. We require u
sends zizi+1 to LK2(ei(v)).

12.3. The case when Lκ = Lκ′ but θκ ̸= θκ′. We consider the case Lκ = Lκ′ but
θκ ̸= θκ′ then we need to replace the de Rham differential d by d+(θκ′ −θκ)∧. (See
(9.25).) We put d′ = d + (θκ′ −θκ)∧. The L2 adjoint of d′ is δ′ = (−)n+np+1 ∗d′∗
on p forms. Hence we replace the usual Laplacian by ∆′ = −d′δ′ −δ′d′ and use
Harmonic analysis in the same way. Thus we can obtain Πκ,κ′, Gκ,κ′ satisfying
a similar condition as Condition 12.2. We use them to modify the argument of
Subsection 12.1 in an obvious way65 to include such cases.
The image of Πκ,κ′ is the cohomology H∗(˜Lκ; d′), that is, the de Rham coho-
mology with local coefficient. Therefore H∗(˜Lκ; d′) ⊗F Λ0 is (the diagonal part of)
the underlying module of L((Lκ, θκ), (Lκ′, θκ′)).66 The proof of Theorem 1.1 is now
complete.
□
We remark that we assumed that i∗
L[b2] = 0 ∈H2(L; F). (This is necessary for
the connection θL of the trivial bundle to exist.) On the other hand, the cohomology
class i∗
L[b+] ∈H2(L; Λ+) is not assumed to be zero. In fact sometimes we use non-
trivial i∗
L[b+] ∈H2(L; Λ+) to cancel the obstruction class m0(1) (for the existence
of the bounding cochains). (This is the way to show that if i∗
L : Heven(X; F) →
Heven(L; F) is surjective then L is unobstructed after suitable bulk deformation.
See [FOOO1, Theorem C].)

In [FOOO6], two formulas (12.24) and (12.25) played very important roles.

ring is a ring homomorphism. (12.25) is used to show a relation between criticality
of the potential function and non-vanishing of Lagrangian Floer cohomology. We
will go back to the discussion of this point at Subsection 28.5.

st. Let L be a finite collection {(Lκ, θκ, bκ) | κ = 1, 2, . . . }.

Proposition 12.9. Suppose we are given choices to define the structure of unital
cyclic filtered A∞category L with L as the set of objects and U with U as the set
of objects.
Then we can define the structure of unital cyclic filtered A∞category with L∪U
as the set of objects, extending the given structures.
The proof is immediate from the way of constructing unital cyclic filtered A∞
structures.

times already in this paper, we can modify it to prove Proposition 12.10.

We next use Proposition 12.10 to prove the uniqueness of the canonical model
L. Such a uniqueness is discussed in [Fu2, Section 12]. Actually one point is not
discussed there. It is independence of the harmonic projection Π and the propagator
(Green kernel) G. (See [Fu2, Remark 12.1].) In [HY, Section 7], Hang Yuan proved
such uniqueness up to pseudo-isotopy. He did not discuss cyclic symmetry there.
However, actually we can easily adapt the argument of [HY] to complete the proof
of the next uniqueness result:
Theorem 12.11. The cyclic and unital filtered A∞category L is independent of
various choice involved (including almost complex structures) up to cyclic and unital
pseudo-isotopy.

Remark 12.15. In Subsection 12.2, we prove that there exists a cyclic filtered A∞
functor from L to Lform
c.u. which is a quasi-isomorphism. Unfortunately it does not
so immediately imply the independence of L up to cyclic homotopy equivalence. In
fact at the time of writing of this paper the authors do not know whether existence
of homotopy inverse of a quasi-isomorphism (a version of Whitehead theorem for
A∞category) holds in the cyclic category.
13. Construction of Kuranishi structures.
In this and the next sections, we construct (systems of) Kuranishi structures
and CF-perturbations thereon which are used in the previous sections of Part 2.
Similar arguments are used to construct (systems of) Kuranishi structures and CF-
perturbations appearing in Part 3. The arguments are rather technical and make
heavy use of the tool kits developed by 2nd-5th authors.
Readers who are not
interested in such detail can skip this section and the next.
In this section we prove Propositions 7.3, 9.18, 10.5 and 10.7 which claim exis-
tence of Kuranishi structures on various moduli spaces involved. We employ the
strategy established in [FOOO13, FOOO14, FOOO16]. In fact, except Items (8),
Proposition 7.3 was proved in [FOOO13, FOOO14], in mostly the same way as
this section. (The method taken in this paper to achieve the compatibility at the
boundary is different from [FOOO14] but is similar to [FOOO16].)
13.1. Construction of Kuranishi structures of the moduli spaces of disks:
review. In this subsection, we review the construction in [FOOO13, FOOO14] and
in the next subsection we explain how we modify it so that Items (8), (9), (11) are
satisfied.
We consider the situation of Section 7, that is, we have a single embedded La-
grangian submanifold L. The set Xℓ;k+1(L; β), which we call an ambient set, is
defined in the same way as Mℓ;k+1(L; β) is defined in Definition 7.1, except we do
not require u to be pseudo-holomorphic. We do not define topology etc. on it and
regard it as a set. The inclusion Mℓ;k+1(L; β) ⊂Xℓ;k+1(L; β) is obvious.
Suppose a pair (X, M) of a metrizable space M and a set X which contains it
is given.
Definition 13.1. ([FOOO13, Definition 4.1]]) A partial topology of such a pair, by
definition, assigns Bϵ(X, p) ⊂X for each ϵ > 0 and p ∈M, such that:

if the notion of neighborhood coincides.

Theorem 13.3. ([FOOO13, Theorem 7.1]) To an obstruction bundle data of the
moduli space Mℓ,k+1(X, L, J; β) we can associate a Kuranishi structure on Mℓ,k+1(X, L, J; β).
Moreover the Kuranishi structure obtained is canonical in the sense of germs
of Kuranishi structures.74 By choosing appropriate obstruction bundle data, the
evaluation maps become weakly submersive with respect to the Kuranishi structure
obtained. (See Definition 13.26.)
Construction of the Kuranishi structure in Theorem 13.3 roughly goes as follows.
Let p ∈Mℓ,k+1(X, L, J; β). Its Kuranishi neighborhood (an orbifold) Up is the
set of all x ∈Up such that ∂ux ∈Ep(x). Condition (3) implies that we can apply
the implicit function theorem to this equation. Then together with Condition (2) we
find that Up has a structure of orbifold. The obstruction bundle Ep (an orbibundle
on Up) is defined so that the fiber at x is Ep(x). The Kuranishi map sp (a section
of Ep) is defined by sp(x) = ∂ux. Condition (2) implies that it is a smooth section.
If sp(x) = 0 then ux is pseudo-holomorphic.
Namely x ∈Mℓ,k+1(X, L, J; β).
Thus we obtain a parametrization map ψp : s−1
p (0) →Mℓ,k+1(X, L, J; β). The

To obtain a Kuranishi structure as in Propositions 7.3, we need to show an
existence of appropriate obstruction bundle data.
An important point is that,

bundle data so that Propositions 7.3 (5) (and others except (8), (9), (11)) is satisfied.
This was worked out in [FOOO14].
An additional argument needed to show Propositions 7.3 (8) is explained in the
next subsection.

Now to complete the proof of Proposition 7.3 except Item (8), it suffices to find

13.2. Construction of Kuranishi structure of the moduli space of disks:
compatibility with forgetful map. We now discuss the way to obtain a system
of Kuranishi structures that is compatible with the forgetful maps. The strategy
is as follows. We formulate the notion that a system of obstruction bundle data
is compatible with the forgetful maps. Then we show that if a system of obstruc-
tion bundle data is compatible with forgetful maps then the resulting system of
Kuranishi structures is compatible with forgetful maps.
We begin with the first step. We consider Mℓ;k+1(L; β) and a forgetful map
forget : Mℓ;k+1(L; β) →Mℓ,0(L; β)
in the case β ̸= 0. (In the case when β = 0 the obstruction bundle is defined to be
0.)

We can also define

Example 13.14. A counter example is the following.
Let p = (Σp,⃗zp, up) ∈
M0,3(L; β) consist of two disk components Σ1, Σ2. zp,0 ∈Σ1 and zp,1, zp,2 ∈∂Σ2.

marked points on the component Σ2 and shrink the components. We thus obtain
a map from Σ1 only. See Figure 20.
We consider x in an arbitrary small neighborhood of p such that the domain
is the same and the map on Σ2 is close to a constant map but is not a constant
map. Then the source curve forget(x) still has two components. (We shrink only
an unstable component to define forget. The automorphism of a disk with non-
constant map is finite even though the map is close to a constant map.) From the
definition of partial topology such an object is not in a neighborhood of p.
The idea to resolve this issue is as follows. We take an obstruction bundle Ep(x)
so that it is 0 on the component where the map is constant. Then if x satisfies the

Definition 13.16. We say that an obstruction bundle data {Ep(x)} on Mℓ;k+1(L; β)

Note that ˆΦp,x depends on various choices such as the local trivialization of the

choices.

Example 13.18. Let us consider the situation of Example 13.14, which is a counter

the domain is the same, namely it has two irreducible components Σ1, Σ2. Let
{Ep(x)} be an obstruction data which satisfies the support preserving property
and preserves triviality. Then it follows from the definition that the support of
an element of Ep(x) does not intersect with Σ2. Suppose ∂ux ∈Ep(x). Then
ux is constant on Σ2. Therefore, after forgetting the marked points the domain of
x = forget(x) is Σ1. (Namely we shrink Σ2.) Therefore x is in a small neighborhood
of p.

14. Construction of CF perturbations.
14.1. A brief review of a CF-perturbation and the integration along the
fiber. This subsection and the next are reviews of [FOOO15] and explain how to
construct a system of CF-perturbations and use it to define the integration along
the fiber. We first briefly review the definition of a CF-perturbation. Let Γ be
a finite group and E →V a Γ equivariant vector bundle on a manifold V with
effective Γ action. It is, by definition, a vector bundle on U = V/Γ. Let s be a
section of E →V . We require that s is smooth and Γ equivariant. We consider the

to the case when f is an evaluation map. We also assume that f is a submersion.
