14.2. Construction of CF-perturbations using outer collaring:
review.
In this subsection we review the proof of Proposition 7.6 except Item (3), the
forgetful map compactibility. The proof was given in [FOOO15, Chapter 22] using
various results in the earlier chapters of [FOOO15] especially its Chapter 17. In
this subsection we review it.
Proposition 7.6 is an existence theorem of a system of CF-perturbations. We
can prove it by using two basic existence theorems, which we will explain below in
order.
The first existence theorem is an analogy of the standard transversality theorem
for sections of vector bundles on manifolds. Let E →M be a smooth vector bundle
on a smooth manifold. Let K ⊂M be a compact subset. Suppose there exists a
section s0 of E on a neighborhood of K which is transversal to 0. Then there exists
a smooth section s of E on M which is transversal to 0 and s = s0 on K.
We want to obtain a version of this classical result for a CF-perturbation of a
Kuranishi structure. To prove such a variant, we need to employ a good coordinate
system rather than a Kuranishi structure. So we first prove the result by employing
a good coordinate system compatible to the given Kuranishi structure and obtain
a good coordinate system version of CF-perturbation. We then go back to the Ku-
ranishi structure to obtain a (Kuranishi structure version of) CF-perturbation. A
technical point to deal with in the process is that the Kuranishi structure which we
extract from a good coordinate system compatible to the given Kuranishi structure
is not the original Kuranishi structure but is its thickening in general. We explain
the notion of thickening below.
We take a short cut using the fact that our Kuranishi structure on the moduli
space M is obtained from an obstruction bundle data {Ep(x)} as we explained in
Subsection 13.1. We denote by bUE∗(∗) the Kuranishi structure obtained from an
obstruction bundle data {Ep(x)}. We then have the next lemma:
Lemma 14.3. Let {Ep(x)} and {E′
p(x)} be two obstruction bundle data which
induce Kuranishi structures bUE∗(∗) and bUE′
∗(∗) on our moduli space M respectively.
We assume:
(*) For each p ∈M there exists a neighborhood Up of p in M such that the
following holds. If q ∈Up and x ∈X is in the ambient set and is in a
sufficiently small neighborhood of q then
Ep(x) ⊆E′
q(x).
(14.3)
If (∗) is satisfied then bUE′
∗(∗) is a thickening of bUE∗(∗) in the sense of [FOOO15,
Definition 5.3].
Once [FOOO15, Definition 5.3] is understood, Lemma 14.3 is easy to prove.
However, for the purpose of this paper, we can (and will) take a short cut and
regard (*) as the definition of thickening.
Remark 14.4.
(1) Semi-continuity of obstruction bundle data (Definition 13.2 (4)) implies
Ep(x) ⊇Eq(x). The direction of inclusion is opposite from (14.3).

Proposition 14.5 is a direct consequence of [FOOO15, Proposition 7.59] using
[FOOO15, Lemmas 9.10, 9.11].80 We omit the proof in this review.
To prove Proposition 7.6 we need another kind of extension result of CF-perturbations,
which we now explain.
The construction of a system of CF-perturbations on
Mℓ,k+1(X, L; β) is by induction on the numbers ω(β), k, ℓ.
The moduli space
Mℓ,k+1(X, L; β) has a boundary and corners (in the sense of Kuranishi structure).
The boundary is described by (7.1).
The corner is described by multiple fiber
product. See [FOOO15, (21.1) and Definition 21.3]. To prove that the obtained
CF-perturabtions are compatible along the boundary and corners, we need to show
the following. The restriction of the CF-perturbation of Mℓ,k+1(X, L; β) to the
boundary coincides with the fiber product CF-perturbations of the boundary and
corners. See [FOOO15, Section 10.2] for the definition of the fiber product CF-
perturbation.
In our situation ev0 is strongly submersive.
Therefore the fiber
product CF-perturbation is well defined by [FOOO15, Lemma-Definition 10.12].
To prove the existence of such CF-perturbations by induction, we need to extend a
CF-perturbation defined at the boundary of Mℓ,k+1(X, L; β) to its neighborhood.
In fact the assumption of Proposition 14.5 requires that the CF-perturbation is
given on a neighborhood of K. The proof of this extendability is somewhat cumber
some.
Let us elaborate on the issue that arises while we give the proof of this extend-
ability. We remark that the CF-perturbation is an equivalence class of (W, {sϵ}, χ).

However we need ensure the following in order for this argument to work:
• We can choose a representative (Wp, {sp,ϵ}, χp) that extends to a neighbor-

It is important to take a single representative which extends to both fiber products
simultaneously. Since the equivalence relation between CF-perturbations is not so

A way to go around this trouble is to utilize the collar of the boundary and cor-
ners. Let us consider again the element p above. It is contained in the codimension
2 corner which is identified with the fiber product:
M3(X, L; β1) ×L M3(X, L; β2) ×L M3(X, L; β3).

The Kuranishi neighborhood of p in the two fiber products in (14.4) can be
identified with Up × {0} × [0, 1) and Up × [0, 1) × {0}, respectively. Suppose the CF
perturbation on them are both collared. It means that there exists τ > 0 such that
sp,ϵ((x, t, 0), ξ) = sp,ϵ((x, 0, 0), ξ) and sp,ϵ((x, 0, t), ξ) = sp,ϵ((x, 0, 0), ξ) for t < τ.
Here x ∈Up, ξ ∈W. We can then extend it to a neighborhood of Up × {(0, 0)} in
Up × [0, 1) × [0, 1) by defining
sp,ϵ((x, t1, t2), ξ) = sp,ϵ((x, 0, 0), ξ)
for t1, t2 ∈[0, τ).
This way of extension is simpler and works better with the
equivalence relation of CF-perturbations.
The problem is the way to find a global collar to a Kuranishi structure. The
existence of a collar of a given manifold with corners is classical.82 In the case
of Kuranishi structure an issue occurs since collar of a manifold with corners is
not enough canonical. (See [FOOO15, Section 18.4] for a discussion on a related
problem.) In the case of a Kuranishi structure, we need to equip each Kuranishi
chart with a collar simultaneously in the collection of Kuranishi charts associated

We next recall the notion of normalized boundary. For a manifold with corners
M its normalized boundary is ∂M which is also a manifold with corners. There is
a map π : ∂M →M. If p is interior of M then π−1(p) is the empty set. If p is
in the boundary of M but is not in the corner, then π−1(p) consists of one point.
If p is in the codimension k corner but not in in the codimension k + 1 corner,
then π−1(p) consists of k points. See [FOOO15, Definition 8.4 and Lemma 8.2].
By taking normalized boundary of each Kuranishi chart, a space with Kuranishi
structure (with a boundary and corners) has a normalized boundary which is again
a Kuranishi structure with a boundary and corners.
In the case of ∂Mℓ;k+1(L; β) its normalized boundary is a disjoint union
[
M#L1;k1+1(L; β1)ev0 ×evi M#L2;k2+1(L; β2).
(14.6)
The disjoint union is taken over all (L1, L2) ∈Shuff(ℓ), i = 1, . . . , k2, k1, k2 ∈Z≥0
with k1 + k2 = k and β1, β2 ∈H2(X, L; Z) with β1 + β2 = β. (Compare (7.1).)
Note that the boundary of (14.6) is
[
M#L1;k1+1(L; β1)ev0 ×evi M#L2;k2+1(L; β2)ev0 ×evj M#L3;k3+1(L; β3). (14.7)
More precisely each element of (14.7) appears twice in the boundary of (14.6). We
say a Kuranishi structure, a CF-perturbation etc. of (14.6) is compatible at the
corner if they coincide at the two points which corresponds to a point in (14.7).
Remark 14.7. Here we say coincide. In the situation of this paper we can safely
say so. In fact a Kuranishi neighborhood is, for example, a subset of the ambient
set.
So whether it coincides or not is a well defined notion.
The same is true
for obstruction bundles, CF-perturbations and etc. In [FOOO15] the boundary
compatibility (See [FOOO15, Condition 16.17 (X)].) requires that two Kuranishi
structures are isomorphic, instead of requiring them to coincide. In the story of
a system of abstract Kuranishi structures which is developed in [FOOO15], we
can not define the notion that two Kuranishi structures coincide. By this reason,
we need to require further consistency of isomorphisms. (See [FOOO15, Remark
15.6].) Such a further consistency condition is the corner compatibility condition.
(See [FOOO15, Condition 16.17 (XI), (XII)].)
Thus the story here is simplified compared to [FOOO15, Chapter 17].

The way to resolve this problem is as follows. (See [FOOO2, pages 777-778].)
For a family of sections sϵ(x, ξ) which is a part of a CF-perturbation, we consider
only those with the properties
|sϵ((T1, T2, x), ξ) −sϵ((∞, ∞, x), ξ)|Ck < Ck exp(−δkTi)
Here we take derivatives with respect to Ti to define the Ck norm. (The param-
eter x of the Kuranishi neighborhood contains those corresponding to the gluing
parameters. So we write x = (T1, T2, x).) Such a function is called admissible in
[FOOO15, Chapter 25]. (See [FOOO15, Definition 25.3] for its precise definition.)


## Page 149


QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY AND SPLIT GENERATION
149
We can define orbifold, vector bundle, sections and etc. in the category of admis-
sible functions and can prove various results on usual smooth functions such as
implicit function theorem etc. in the same way in the admissible category. So the
construction of a Kuranishi structure, CF-perturbations etc. can be carried out in
the same way in the admissible category. (See [FOOO15, Chapter 25].) If a CF-
perturbation etc. is admissible then it is smooth with respect to the coordinates ti
at (t1, t2) = (0, 0). Moreover since T = T1 + T2 is the forgetful map, the pull back
of an admissible CF-perturbation is again admissible.
Another reason to work in the category of admissible functions concerns the
outer collaring. At the boundary or corners, which is given by Ti = ∞, that is,
ti = 0, we attach outer collar that is ti ∈[−1, 0]. The coordinate change maps, CF-
perturbations and etc. are extended to the outer collar so that they are constant
in ti direction. (for ti ∈[0, ϵ).) For this extension to become smooth at ti = 0,
the coordinate change maps, CF-perturbations and etc. must have vanishing ti
derivatives of all order at ti = 0. The coordinate ti does not have this property. The
coordinate 1/Ti has this properties for CF-perturbations because of the estimates
of the form (14.8). For coordinate change maps, we need to take the logarithum
once more and use ti = 1/ log Ti. Then using ti cordinate, the coordinate change
maps, CF-perturbations etc. have vanishing ti derivatives of all order at ti = 0.
Therefore using this coordinate outer collar has a smooth structure. See [FOOO15,
Chapters 25 and 17], [FOOO17] for detail.
Now we discuss the way to pull back the CF-perturbation defined on the outer
collar by the forgetful map forget⊞1.
We introduce a class of CF-perturbations which can be pulled back by the map
forget⊞1. It is called a bi-collared CF-perturbation.
Definition 14.9. Let {Eˆp(bx)} be a τ-bi-collared obstruction bundle data on the
moduli space Mℓ;k+1(L; β)⊞1. It induces a τ-bi-collared Kuranishi structure bUE∗(∗).
A CF-perturbation “
S of bUE∗(∗) is said to be τ-bi-collared if for each bp ∈Mℓ,k+1(X, L; β)⊞1
we can take a representative (Wˆp, {sˆp,ϵ}, χˆp) such that the following holds.
Suppose bx1, bx2 are in a neighborhood of ˆp in the ambient set. We assume they
are in Up the Kuranishi neighborhood of p. If Π′
τ(bx1) = Π′
τ(bx2) then
sp,ϵ(bx1, ξ) = sp,ϵ(bx2, ξ).
Proposition 14.10. Suppose a system of τ-bi-collared obstruction bundle data on
Mℓ;k+1(L; β)⊞1 and on Mℓ;0(L; β)⊞1 are given such that they are compatible with
the forgetful map.
In this situation a τ-bi-collared admissible CF-perturbation on Mℓ;0(L; β)⊞1 can
be pulled back by the forgetful map to a (smooth and) τ-bi-collared admissible CF-
perturbation on Mℓ;k+1(L; β)⊞1.
We say that two CF-perturbations are compatible if one is obtained from the
other via the pull-back.
The notion of pull-back of a CF-perturbation by a forgetful map is the obvious
one. Using the notation of Definition 13.21, the pull back of a CF-perturbation
(W, {sϵ}, χ) on (U 2
p2, E2
p2, ψ2

p1, E1

Proof. The only issue to take care of is the non-smoothness of Mm.
The singular locus of Mm is described in Lemma 13.29 (6), (a) and (b). We
observe that in such a case the τ-bi-collared-ness implies that the CF-perturbation

M. ABOUZAID, K. FUKAYA, Y.-G. OH, H. OHTA, K. ONO
is constant in the si direction. (We use ϵ < τ here.) Therefore, the non-smooth-ness
of Mm does not affect the smooth-ness of the pull back.
□
Now we are in the position to complete the proof of an outer-collared-version
of Proposition 7.6 (3). We observe that the inductive construction of the system
of CF-perturbations, which we described in Subsection 14.2, can be performed in
the category of bi-collared CF-perturbation.
Therefore using Proposition 14.10
we can perform the inductive construction of CF-perturbations compatible with
forgetful map by the induction scheme of the construction of Kuranishi structures
in Subsection 13.2, to obtain a system of CF-perturbations as in Proposition 7.6
including Item (3). (We use the inductive scheme of the proof of Lemma 13.23 so
that the CF-perturbations become bi-collared.)
The proofs of Propositions 10.9, 10.10 etc. are similar. To prove Proposition
10.10 we use Proposition 14.5.

bpb : HH∗(L) →H(X; Λ0).

The idea of the proof is to study the commutator:
[d, H(Γ,v)(T)]

3rd term

Figure 31. 3rd and 4th terms
of H(Γ,v)(T) with the de Rham differential d. Recalling that H(Γ,v)(T) is a com-
position of operators assigned to the vertices and the edges of Γ, this commutator
is expressed as a sum of the terms arising from the replacement of various opera-
tors assigned to the vertices and edges of Γ for H(T) by their commutators with d.
Taking commutators with those assigned to the vertices gives
[d, m]
or
[d, T].
and those assigned to the edges gives
[d, G] = id −Π.
respectively. Note [d, m] = m◦bm by the A∞relation. Therefore the terms replacing
m by [d, m] cancel the terms arising from the replacement of G by id.
On the other hand,91
[d, T] + m ◦bT + T ◦bm = δH(T).
The second and the third terms of the left hand side, cancel with terms replacing
G by id for edges adjacent to the vertex assigned with T.
The terms arising from the replacement of G by Π for edges become the second
and third terms respectively of the right hand side of the formula to be proven.
There is an exceptional case, that is, the case when we replace G with Π or id
for the edge adjacent to the root. They will give J(Γ,v)(T) (the first term) and the
fourth term of the right hand side, respectively.
Thus we have proved the required formula modulo the sign. The fact the equality
holds with sign is a consequence of the fact that all the signs here are by the Koszul
rule.
□

16. The open-closed map bp.
16.1. Basic idea of the construction. In this section we define the open-closed
map bp. We use the same moduli space as the one we used in the definition of bq.
We however regard one of the interior marked points as the output. So we need
to assume that the evaluation map at this marked point is a weak submersion. By
this reason, we need to use a slightly different perturbation from those used for the
definition of bq.
We summarize the properties of the map bp we obtain in the next theorem.
Theorem 16.1. Consider the category L associated to the choices of L and b.
Then there exists a chain map
bpb : CH∗(L, L) →Ω(X)ˆ⊗FΛ0
with the following properties.

Proposition 16.3 (7) is a consequence of Lemma 16.9 (3)′. Proposition 16.3 (8)(9)
are consequences of corresponding symmetry of the obstruction bundle data.
Now we discuss the case when p satisfies (2.a),(2.b),(2.c). This case is similar to
the discussion of [FOOO2, Subsection 7.4.1], which we explain now.
We first consider the simplest case where p ∈M1;∅(κ; 0). Our p is ((D2, 0); ip)
where (D2, 0) is a disk together with the 0-th marked point 0. ip is the constant map
to p ∈Lκ. We put a (non-trivial) obstruction bundle Ep(x) ⊂C∞(u∗
x(TX)⊗Λ0,1).
Here x = ((D2, 0); ux) and ux : (D2, ∂D2) →(X, Lκ) is close to a constant map
but is not necessarily constant. We require ∂ux ∈Ep(x). Since constant maps are
transversal, the set of such maps ux is identified with the product of a neighborhood
U(L) of p in Lκ and a neighborhood of 0 of Ep(p). The group S1 acts trivially
on Ep(p). Therefore Up ∼= U(L) × Bϵ(0; Rm). If this neighborhood is small then
the S1 = Aut(p) action is trivial on Up. A neighborhood of p in M1;∅(κ; 0) is
U(L). We remark that the virtual dimension of M1;∅(κ; 0) is dim L −1 because of
S1 symmetry. On the other hand dim Up is dim L + m and the rank of Ep is m.
Hence dim Up −rank(Ep) = dim L does not coincide with virtual dimension. This
is because S1 action is trivial on Up. So in this case our ‘chart’ is not Kuranishi
chart in the usual sense.
We next consider the case of Mℓ+1;∅(κ; B) with ℓ> 0 or B ̸= 0. Suppose p ∈
Mℓ+1;∅(κ; B) satisfies (2.a),(2.b),(2.c). Then p is represented by ((D2∪Σs;⃗z+), up).
Here Σs is a tree of sphere components attached to 0 ∈D2 and up : D2 ∪Σs →
X is holomorphic such that up(∂D2) ∈Lκ.
up is constant on D2.
Moreover
((Σs;⃗z+), up|Σs) is a stable map in the usual sense. The set of elements with the
same combinatorial type as p is described by the fiber product (16.8).
To obtain a Kuranishi neighborhood we include a gluing parameter to smooth
the double point 0 ∈D2. The set of gluing parameters is identified with a small
neighborhood of 0 in C. The automorphism group S1 of p acts as a linear action of
U(1) = S1 on C, which is the set of gluing parameters. In particular the action is
free. Therefore the actual gluing parameter (appearing in the Kuranishi neighbor-
hood) is Bϵ(C)/S1 ∼= [0, ϵ). Thus Up is an orbifold with boundary. Since S1 action
on Ep(p) is trivial Ep is a well defined orbibundle on Up. Thus in this case we have
a Kuranishi neighborhood in the usual sense. See [FOOO2, Subsection 7.4.1] for
the detailed argument of this case.
□
Remark 16.13. Note that we need to include non-trivial obstruction bundle Ep(x)
for p ∈M1;∅(κ; 0).
This is because otherwise the evaluation map at the 0-th
interior marked point is not weakly submersive. This point is a big difference from
the case of q-perturbation.
In that case we need the weak submersivity of the


## Page 173


QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY AND SPLIT GENERATION
173
evaluation map at the 0-th boundary marked point. The moduli space M0;1(κ; 0)
is transversal and is identified with ˜Lκ. Therefore we do not need to put nontrivial
obstruction bundle but have only to put zero there so that the evaluation map
M0;1(κ; 0) →˜Lκ becomes weakly submersive. This point was very important to
make our system of q-Kuranishi structures compatible with the forgetful map of
boundary marked points.
We remark that we put the q-Kuranishi structure on the disk bubbles for the
moduli spaces in Proposition 16.3 (which we use to define the operator p). There-
fore we equip the trivial obstruction bundle with the constant disk bubbles. On the
other hand, we equip the p-Kuranishi obstruction bundle data with the extended
disk component which contains the 0-th interior marked point. The latter obstruc-
tion bundle data is non-trivial on this extended disk component where the map is
constant.
Now to prove Proposition 16.3 we need to take the obstruction bundle data
(which induces p-Kuranishi structure) so that it is compatible with the fiber product
description of the boundary. This step is similar to and easier than the case of q
perturbation and so is omitted. We however explain the compatibility of Kuranishi
structures with the forgetful map in the case ⃗k \ fe = ∅= ff, since this case is new
in our situation. In this case we need to define a forgetful map
forget : Mℓ+1;⃗k(((κ, . . . , κ), ∅); B;⃗f) →Mℓ+1;∅(κ; B).
(16.9)
(Here ∅in ((κ, . . . , κ), ∅) means that ⃗p is empty.) We took the obstruction data of
the target. We define the obstruction data of the domain so that it is compatible
with this forgetful map. Then the induced Kuranishi structures are compatible
with forgetful map.
In case ℓ= 0 and B = 0, the ‘chart’ we have on M1;∅(κ; 0) is not a Kuranishi
chart. However we do have obstruction bundle data and all the elements of the
obstruction bundle are fixed by the S1 action. (Lemma 16.9 (6)′). Therefore we
can pull back obstruction bundle data.

Remark 16.19. We remark that in the construction of closed-open map q, we use a
variant of the Hochschild cochain complex, that is, those consisting of elements with
Schwartz kernels satisfying Condition 15.8. This is a subcomplex of the algebraic
Hochschild cochain complex. Here, during the construction of open-closed map p,
we use a variant of Hochschild chain complex, where we replace the algebraic tensor
product Ω(L)⊗Ω(L′) of Ω(L) and Ω(L′) by its completion Ω(L×L′). So our variant
contains the algebraic Hochschild chain complex as a subcomplex. In other words,
the direction of inclusion is opposite. This fact is consistent with the fact that
Hochschild cochain complex is the target of the closed-open map and Hochschild
chain complex is the domain of the open-closed map.

We have thus proved a de Rham complex version of Theorem 16.1. We have also

Proposition 16.23. The maps bpf.c.u.b and bpf.u.b are the linear parts of L∞module
homomorphisms.
We used the homotopy inverse limit to construct a chain map by utilizing the
fact that the existence of the promotion of the chain complex depends only on the
chain homotopy type. To prove Proposition 16.23 we use appropriate obstruction
theory to show that the existence of the promotion of the Lℓmodule structure
depends only on homotopy type. We omit the detail here and referring readers to
[FOOO2, Section 7.2.12].

Wrap up of the proof of Theorem 16.1. The properties claimed in Theorem 16.1 is
a consequence of corresponding properties of bpf.u.b. The bpf.u.b version of Theorem
16.1 (2) follows from the compatibility of the forgetful maps with the Kuranishi
structures (Proposition 16.3 (5)) and of CF-perturbations (Proposition 16.14 (3)).
The cyclic symmetry (Theorem 16.1 (3)) is a consequence of Proposition 16.3 (9)
and Proposition 16.14 (7). Theorem 16.1 (1) is a consequence of the description
of local chart on M1;∅(κ; 0) given during the proof of Lemma 16.11. The proof of
Theorem 16.1 is complete.
□
Remark 16.26. It seems likely that we can prove that the chain map bfH in Def-
inition 16.24 is the linear part of an L∞module homomorpism over the L∞ho-
momorphism mentioned in Remark 15.25. It will imply the de Rham cohomology
version of Proposition 16.23. We do not try to prove it here since we do not use it
in this paper. See [FOOO2, Theorem 7.4.186] for a related result.
Remark 16.27. By the construction of this section, it becomes possible to work
with the filtered A∞category where morphism spaces are finite dimensional Λ
vector spaces in Part 4 and etc. So taking a completion of the tensor products
among them becomes unnecessary.

17. Duality between bq and bp.

18. Nontriviality of bq and bp.
In this subsection we prove the following:
Proposition 18.1. If the Floer cohomology HF((U, bU), (U, bU); Λ) with Novikov
field Λ coefficient is nonzero, then
bp(b,bU) : HH∗(HF((U, bU), (U, bU); Λ)) →H(X; Λ)
is nonzero.
Here U is a relatively spin Lagrangian submanifold in X and bU is its bounding
cochain. We consider the category with only one object (U, bU) and the Hochschild
homology in the left hand side is that of this category.
We remark that we use the Novikov field Λ rather than Λ0 as a coefficient in
Proposition 18.2. In fact, the non-triviality over Λ is stronger that the non-triviality
over Λ0.

remark that the complex defining Hochschild cohomology HH∗(HF((U, bU), (U, bU); Λ))

19. ˆq is a ring homomorphism.
In this section we prove:
Theorem 19.1. ˆqb : QH∗
q(X; Λ0) →HH∗(L, L) is a unital ring homomorphism.
The degree of ˆqb is given by: deg(ˆqb(g)) = deg g −1.102 Therefore
deg(ˆqb(g)) = deg g.
In other words, ˆqb is degree preserving if we use deg for Hochschild cohomology.
(See (4.60) and footnotes 20, 21 for deg and deg.) Since the cup product ∪on
Hochschild cohomology is associative and degree preserving using deg, this fact is
consistent with Theorem 19.1.
Remark 19.2. We refer to [FOOO6, Section 4.7] for some of the history of this
and related results.
Remark 19.3. Theorem 19.1 and Corollary 15.26 should be combined and be
a part of the statement that closed-open map is a morphism over a little-disc-
operad. We do not to try to prove it in this paper. Note that the existence of
module structure over little-disc-operad on Hochschild cohomology is called Deligne
conjecture and is proved by several people ([KS2, MS, Ta, Vo]).
Remark 19.4. In order to handle infinitely many moduli spaces at the same time
there occurs a problem similar to the one mentioned in Remark 7.4. In the situation
of this and the next sections however, we will need to study only finitely many of
them. In fact the statement we prove here is an equality between two operators
that are independent of various choices involved. So if we prove that they coincide
modulo T E for each given E then the equality holds. So we have to study only the
moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic maps of symplectic area ≤Ei for a sequence
Ei going to +∞. In this and the next sections we do not explicitly mention that
we consider finitely many moduli spaces only in order to simplify the description.
19.1. The chain level formula. We first prove the following chain level version
of Theorem 19.1.
Proposition 19.5. The chain map ˆqf.u.b induces a ring homomorphism QH∗
q(X; Λ0) →
HH∗(Lf.u.b, Lf.u.b).
Proof. The proof is similar to the proof of [FOOO6, Theorem 2.6.1] which dealt
with the toric case and proceeds as follows.
Consider the moduli space Mℓ+2;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p); B) introduced in Definition 9.15. Among
the ℓ+2 interior marked points we handle the first and the second ones in a different
way from others. We denote them by z+
I and z+
II. Other interior marked points are
denoted by z+
1 , . . . , z+
ℓ. We add data to specify 0-th boundary marked point and
define Mℓ+2;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p, m); B).
102In [FOOO1, Theorem 3.8.32] the degree is claimed to be +1. Note in [FOOO1] the degree
is shifted by 2 for H∗(X; Λ0) and deg is used for Hochschild cohomology. Therefore it is consistent
with the degree given here.

Let M1;2 be the compactified moduli space of genus zero bordered Riemann
surface with connected boundary, one boundary marked point and 2 interior marked
points. The moduli space M1;2 can be identified with a 2-disk D2. We describe
three of its special points. See Figure 34.

Mℓ+2;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p, m); B). Namely (Σ,⃗z+,⃗z) is a genus zero bordered Riemann surface
with one boundary component and marked points ⃗z+,⃗z, and u : Σ →X is a
pseudo-holomorphic map. We assume the boundary condition determined by (⃗κ, ⃗p)
and that u is of homology class B. We forget all the 3rd - (ℓ+2)nd interior marked
points except the first two and all the boundary marked points except the 0-th one.
We also forget the map u. Then we obtain (Σ, (z+
I , z+

component. We shrink them in an obvious way and obtain an element of M1;2.

structure on Mℓ+2;⃗k((⃗κ, ⃗p, m); B) that satisfies appropriate compatibility properties
at [Σ0], [Σ′], [Σ′′]. We describe the compatibility condition below after recalling the
description of the relevant spaces of stable maps.

we can use marked points labelled by fe and/or ff in the same way as Subsection
10.3.) The sign ✠can be defined in a similar way as the beginning of Section 21.
It is straightforward to see that (19.10) induces the required chain homotopy.
The proof of Proposition 19.5 is complete.
□
19.2. Formula on canonical model. We next prove the same conclusion for the
canonical model. Let g1, g2 be de Rham cycles in X. We define g1 ∪b g2 by (19.4).
Proposition 19.5 implies that the difference
bqf.u.b(g1 ∪b g2) −bqf.u.b(g1) ∪bqf.u.b(g2)
is a Hochschild coboundary. Therefore to complete the proof of Theorem 19.1 it
suffices to prove the next lemma.
Lemma 19.17. The map J in Lemma 15.23 induces a ring homomorphism between
Hochschild cohomologies.

20. The Cardy relation.
In this section we define the moduli space of pseudo-holomorphic annuli, which
is the key ingredient in the proofs of Theorems 1.7 and 1.8 and discuss its basic
properties.
20.1. Inner product Z among Hochschild homologies. To state the conclu-
sion of this section, that is, the Cardy relation, we first need to consider a pairing
on Hochschild homology, which we denote by Z = Z(x, y).108
Explanation of
the construction of this pairing is in order. As before we fix a bulk class b and
a background class st ∈H2(X; Z2).
Then let L = {(Lκ, θκ, bκ) | κ1, . . . , κK}
be a collection of st-relatively spin pairwise transversal Lagrangian submanifolds
enhanced by weak bounding cochains. We denote by L the cyclic filtered A∞cate-
gory which they generate as in Section 12. We also consider another such collection
U = {(Uυ, θυ, bυ) | υ = 1, . . . , R} and denote by U the associated cyclic filtered
A∞-category.
We also assume the following:
Assumption 20.1. For all κ and υ, Lκ is transversal to Uυ.
We will define a pairing
Z : HH∗(L; Λ0) ⊗HH∗(U; Λ0) →Λ0,

Remark 20.5. The extra sign (−1)(n+1) deg′ y = (−1)(n+1) deg′ x may be under-

⟨⟩PDX, p, x, p, y 7→Z, x, y
Note ⟨⟩PDX has degree −2n, p has degree n+1 (using deg′ x as the domain degree)
and Z has degree 0. So we might identify ⟨⟩PDX, p, p with Z to define the Koszul
sign.
Remark 20.6. The Poincar´e duality of de Rham cohomology of X is anti sym-
metric with respect the grading. p has degree n from Hochschild homology (with
our degree convention) to the ambient homology. So in case p is an isomorphism
(20.15) implies that Z has symmetry
Z(x, y) = (−1)(n+deg′ x+1)(n+deg′ y+1)Z(y, x).
(20.10)
In [FOOO6] we studied the case when x = y is a delta form supported at a point.
In this case since n + deg′ x + 1 ≡0 mod 2, Z is symmetric. Since toric manifolds
have only even degree cohomology the Poincar´e duality is symmetric. Note that in
case for example the Potential function is Morse, the Hochschild homology of A∞
category splits into 1 dimensional factors which are orthogonal to each other with
respect to Z. So Z must be symmetric.

20.2. The main theorem of section 20.
Theorem 20.8. (Cardy relation) Under the Assumption 20.1 we have
⟨bp(x),bp(y)⟩PDX = (−1)n(n−1)/2Z(x, y),
(20.15)
for [x] ∈HH∗(L; Λ0), [y] ∈HH∗(U; Λ0).
Remark 20.9.
(1) The conclusion of Theorem 20.8 holds without Assumption 20.1. We can
prove it by perturbing Uυ so that Assumption 20.1 is satisfied.
(2) In case L = U = {L(u)} and x = y = PD([pt]) ∈HF((L(u), b), (L(u), b))
Theorem 20.8 coincides with [FOOO6, Proposition 3.5.2]. In this case Def-
inition 20.4 becomes [FOOO6, Definition 1.3.22].
The proof of Theorem 20.8 is based on the study of moduli space of pseudomor-
phic annuli and occupies the rest of this section. The proof is based on the annulus
argument which appeared previously in [Ab, BC, FOOO6] in the literature of sym-
plectic topology. The Cardy relation appeared earlier in the physics literature such
as in [CL].

We say that (u; Σ;⃗z1,⃗z2,⃗z +) is a stable map if its automorphism group is of finite
order.

Let π2(X;⃗κ, ⃗υ, ⃗p, ⃗q) be the set of homotopy classes of the maps satisfying the
conditions (1), (2), (3) and (4) above. Using the Maslov index (in other words the
virtual dimension of the moduli space) and the energy we define an equivalence
relation on π2(X;⃗κ, ⃗υ, ⃗p, ⃗q), and define Π2(X;⃗κ, ⃗υ, ⃗p, ⃗q) to be the quotient by this
equivalence relation, so that two homotopy classes are identified if they have the
same energy and Maslov index. We take forgetability data L⃗f = (Lfe, Lff), U⃗f =
(Ufe, Uff).

Here M(1,1);0 is the moduli space of bordered Riemann surfaces of genus zero
with two boundary components and one marked point in each component of the
boundary.
The map (20.19) is defined by
forgetsc(u; Σ;⃗z1,⃗z2,⃗z +) = (Σ, z1(0), z2(0)),
when the space Σ is defined as follows.
We consider the bordered Riemann surface Σ together with two marked points
z1(0), z2(0) on the boundary. If (Σ; z1(0), z2(0)) is stable then Σ = Σ. If not we
shrink the unstable components of Σ to obtain Σ. We then get (Σ, z1(0), z2(0)) ∈
M(1,1);0.
It is well-known (and is proved in [Ab, Section C.4] and [FOOO6, Lemma 3.4.5])
that M(1,1);0 is a 2 disk. It has a stratification by the combinatorial type of its
elements (Σ, z1(0), z2(0)).
The 0-dimensional stratum consists of two particular
points which we denote by (Σ1; z1

(7) At the point where a coordinate of [0, 1]|Lff |+|Uff | becomes 0 or 1, we require

datas associated to each of the boundary components.
Remark 20.12. By the same reason as we mentioned in Remark 16.4, it is simpler
to state cyclic symmetry in our situation than, for example, in Proposition 9.18 (7).

Lemma 20.14. We may choose the Kuranishi structure of the space [0, 1]|Lff |+|Uff |×
Mann
(⃗κ,⃗k,m1),(⃗υ,⃗r,m2);ℓ(B; ⃗p, ⃗q; L⃗f, U⃗f)⊞1 so that it is compatible with the equality (20.31).
We remark that the union of the right hand side of (20.31) is not a disjoint union.
In this case each member of the union has a Kuranishi structure with corners and
they are glued along the boundaries and corners. The precise description of it is
the same as that of [FOOO6, Lemmas 2.6.22 and 3.4.14]. We refer the readers to
the proof of [FOOO6, Lemma 2.6.22] for the proof of Lemma 20.14.
We next discuss CF-perturbations.
Lemma 20.15. We can define a system of CF-perturbations on [0, 1]|Lff |+|Uff | ×
Mann
(⃗κ,⃗k,m1),(⃗υ,⃗r,m2);ℓ(B; ⃗p, ⃗q; L⃗f, U⃗f)⊞1 so that the following holds:
(1) They are transversal to 0.
(2) They are compatible with the description of the boundary in Lemma 20.11
(3).
(3) They are compatible with the forgetful maps forgetting the marked points
labelled by Lfe, Ufe.
(4) The compatibility of Proposition 20.11 (7) is enhanced to the compatibility
of CF-perturbations.
(5) The CF-perturbations are compatible with the identifications in Lemmas
20.13 and 20.14.
(6) The CF-perturbations are invariant under the permutation of interior marked
points.
(7) The CF-perturbations are invariant under the cyclic permutation of the data
in the same sense as Lemma 20.11 (7).
We later use this CF-perturbation to integrate the forms pulled back by evalua-
tion maps.

Lemma 21.13. The difference between the two orientations is (−1)µ(B).
Proof. Recall our orientation convention for forgetsc([Σ]) given at (21.7), (21.8). In
Propositions 21.9, 21.10 and 21.11, we give orientations on the moduli spaces in the
same way. Namely, the moduli space M4, which is the stable curve compactification
of {(D2; z+, z1(0), z−, z2(0))}/PSL(2, R) is put between TuHol(B) and Rk(1) ⊕Rk(2).
Hence the boundary orientation of the modul space of pseudo-holomorphic poly-
gons of holomogy class B and the orientation on which we use so far differs by the
effect of switching TuHol(B), which is µ(B)-dimensional and the moduli space M4,
which is one-dimensional. Hence we obtain the lemma.
□

Part 4. Applications.
22. Introduction to Part 4.
In this part except Section 28 we do not use the T-adic topology of the Novikov
field or of modules over it: Λ is regarded just as a field without any particular
topology. The filtered A∞category we study is linear over Λ and the morphism
space is a finite dimensional vector space.
So we do not need to (and do not)
take a completion of the tensor products among them. We remark that we have
constructed such a filtered A∞category from Lagrangian Floer theory in this paper
through the following procedure:
(1) We fix a bulk class b = b0+b2+b+ on X, a background class st ∈H2(X, Z2),
and consider a finite collection of Lagrangians L equipped with primitives
θL of the restriction of the class b2 ∈H2(X; F), as well as st-relative spin
structures.
(2) Fixing an appropriate discrete monoid G ⊂R≥0, we construct, for each n, k,
a G-gapped filtered An,k category with these objects. The morphism spaces
are the de Rham complexes of intersections of Lagrangian submanifolds.
(They are infinite dimensional.)
(3) We next take the homotopy limit as (n, k) →∞, yielding a cyclic and
homotopically unital curved filtered A∞category which is linear over Λ0.
The morphism space is a T-adic completion of the tensor product of the de
Rham complex with Λ0.
(4) We turn on bounding cochains. Then we obtain a cyclic and homotopically
unital weakly curvature free filtered A∞category whose objects are given by
an element of the given collection of Lagrangians (with its data of primitives
θL and st-relative Spin structures), together with a choice of bounding
cochain.
(5) We go to the canonical model. Thus we obtain a cyclic, unital and weakly
curvature free filtered A∞category which is linear over Λ0. We next change
the coefficient ring to Λ. Then the morphism spaces are the cohomology
groups of intersections of Lagrangian submanifolds over Λ. (In particular
they are finite dimensional.) At this stage we can forget the T-adic topology
of Λ.
Constructions of the open-closed map ˆp and the closed-open map ˆq are given
by induction in the same way, so that the domains are given by the Hochschild
homology and the Hochschild cohomology of the A∞category obtained in Step (5).
Note that the target of closed-open map ˆq is Hochschild cohomology where we take
direct product of the Λ-vector spaces Hom(BkL, L) with various k. The domain of
the open-closed map ˆp is a Hochschild homology that is a direct sum in that sense.
They are dual each other, in the usual way that the dual to a direct product is a
direct sum.
Our A∞category is divided into orthogonal summands according to the values of
the potential function. The Hochschild cohomology (resp. homology) groups that
we consider are given by the direct product (resp. sum) of the groups associated
to each summand. When we consider the A∞category associated to a given value
of the potential function we re-define m0 so that it vanishes (it used to be equal
to the product of the unit by the potential value.) In particular, this category has
vanishing curvature.


## Page 246

M. ABOUZAID, K. FUKAYA, Y.-G. OH, H. OHTA, K. ONO
However we note that we have to deal with a (curved) bi-module over objects
with different values of potential function, that is, the morphism space among them.
This plays some role in Section 23.
Notation 22.1. As in the previous sections, let L and U be collections of La-
grangian submanifolds equipped with brane data, which respectively have potential
values λL and λU. Here brane data consist of a relative spin structure, θL and a
bounding cochain.
We write L, U in place of L ⊗Λ0 Λ, U ⊗Λ0 Λ in Part 4.
We sometimes omit the symbol b in the notation λL, λU and etc. We also write
bp (resp. bq) in place of bpb or bpb (resp. bpb or bqb).
In Part 4 we write CF(L, L′) etc. for CF can(L, L′). For example, when L = L′
the group CF(L, L) denotes the (co)homology group H(L; Λ) and not the de Rham
complex.
23. Proof of Theorem 1.7.
By the results of Subsection 12.6, there is a cyclic and unital A∞category, which
we denote by F and which admits fully faithful embeddings
L →F ←U
(23.1)
of categories whose objects are Lagrangians in L and U. In particular, restricting
the diagonal bi-module (see Subsection 4.3) of F to L on the left and to U on the
right (or vice versa), defines and U −L-bi-module and a L −U-bidmodule which
we respectively denote by UFL and LFU. Omitting the auxiliary data from the
notation, the Λ vector spaces underlying these bi-modules are respectively
UFL(U, L) = CF ∗(U, L)
(23.2)
LFU(L, U) = CF ∗(L, U).
(23.3)

and bi-modules. This means that an element x of CF ∗(U, L) will be considered to
have degree deg′(x) as an element of the value of the bi-module UFL at the pair
(U, L).

Remark 23.3. Putting aside the different technical assumptions, this result should
be interpreted as a generalisation and an extension of Proposition 1.3 of [Ab] which
studied the case of exact Lagrangians in Liouville manifolds. The symplectic coho-
mology of the ambient space replaces, in that situation, the quantum cohomology.
The main improvement is that the Lagrangians in [Ab] are assumed, a priori, to
have vanishing potential value. In addition, only the analogue of the composition
q ◦ˆp: HH∗−n(L, L) →HF ∗(U, bU)
(23.6)

Here, the map q from QH∗(X; Λ) to the Lagrangian Floer cohomology of each
object of U factors through ˆq.
Corollary 23.4. If λL ̸= λU, the composition ˆq ◦ˆp in Equation (23.5) vanishes.
Proof. From the commutativity of the diagram in Equation (23.5), it suffices to
prove that the Hochschild cohomology of the U bi-module UFL⊗
L
LFU vanishes. In
fact, we shall prove that for every pair of objects U and V in U, the complex in
Equation (23.4) is acyclic. To prove this, we consider the length filtration on this
complex. The E1-page of the associated spectral sequence consists of the direct
sum of the groups
CF ∗(U, L0) ⊗CF ∗(L0, L1) ⊗· · · ⊗CF ∗(Ld−1, Ld) ⊗CF ∗(Ld, V ).
(23.7)
and the differential on this page is the tensor product of the differentials. As this is
the tensor product of matrix factorisations with potential values λU−λL, 0, λL−λU
and λL ̸= λU, the cohomology necessarily vanishes, as we proved in Lemma 3.6.
We conclude that the bi-module UFL⊗
L
LFU is acyclic whenever λU ̸= λL.
□
The argument for Theorem 1.7 can now be completed:
Proof of Theorem 1.7. Since q is a ring homomorphism by Theorem 19.1 and q
sends the unit of QH∗(X; Λ) to the unit of HF ∗((U, bU), (U, bU)), see Proposition
18.2 and [FOOO1, (3.8.36.2)], the non-vanishing of the self-Floer cohomology of a
Lagrangian is equivalent to the non-vanishing of the image of the identity idX under
q. Under the assumption of Theorem 1.7, the Hochschild homology of L splits into
the contribution of the different potential values. This is the consequence of the
vanishing of the cohomology of (23.7) we explained above.
Therefore there must be some value λ such that the composition
q ◦ˆp: HH∗(Lλ, Lλ) →HF((U; (b, bU)), (U; (b, bU)); Λ)
(23.8)
does not vanish. Corollary 23.4 implies that this can only happen if λ = POb(U; (b, bU)),
which proves the desired result.
□

the above isomorphisms, we conclude from Theorem 23.2 that ˆp is necessarily in-
jective.
If the ambient symplectic manifold has vanishing first Chern class, the
rank of QH0(M; Λ) is 1, and that of HH0(L, L) is at least one, so that Gana-
tra automatically concludes that the existence of a smooth sub-category L of the
category of Lagrangians implies that L split-generates. In general, one obtains a
split-generation result whenever the rank of the even part of QH∗(X; Λ) is not
larger than that of the even part of HH∗(L, L).

24. Proof of Theorem 1.8.
In this section we shall derive Theorem 1.8 from the precise version of Theorem
23.2 stated as Lemma 23.9. We start with a category L satisfying the assumption
of Theorem 1.7, and let Lλ denote the subcategory of objects whose potential value
is λ. As explained in Remark 1.9, the proof of Theorem 1.8 reduces to the fact
that every unobstructed Lagrangian with the potential value λ is quasi-isomorphic
to a summand of a twisted complex in Lλ. The relevant twisted complex has been
introduced in Section 5.2: starting with the inclusion of categories Lλ ⊂F we
defined, for each integer D, and each object X of F, a twisted complex T D
Lλ(X) in
Tw(Lλ):
Proposition 24.1. There is an integer D with the following property: any pair
(U, bU) of Lagrangian submanifold U and its weak bounding cochain bU with poten-
tial value λ is a summand of T D
Lλ(U, bU).
The assumption of Theorem 1.7 is that the unit in the Quantum cohomology
ring of X lies in the image of ˆp. Since the Hochschild complex has an ascending
filtration by length, this implies the existence of some integer D such that the unit
in fact lies in the image of the composition

25. Proof of Theorem 1.14.
We shall prove Theorem 1.14 in two parts. First, in Subsection 25.1, we use
the results of Section 19 to show that ˆp is surjective. In the next two subsections,
we develop further applications of the annulus argument, by elaborating on the
commutative diagram in Equation (23.5). Finally, in Subsection 25.4, we prove
that ˆp is injective, which completes the proof of Theorem 1.14.
25.1. Surjectivity of ˆp. Recall, from Subsection 4.8, that the Hochschild coho-
mology of L is an algebra, over which Hochschild homology is naturally a module.

Proof. Let τX ∈HH∗(L, L) denote the class whose image under ˆp is eX.

25.4. Injectivity of ˆp. In this subsection, we complete the proof of Theorem 1.14
by proving:
Proposition 25.8. Under the assumptions of Theorem 1.7, the map ˆp is injective
and ˆq is surjective.
In order to prove that ˆp is injective, let us consider, for each pair Lagrangian
L ∈L, a Hamiltonian isotopic copy, which we denote L′, and we now fix U to be a
collection of such Lagrangians. We use this Hamiltonian isotopy to transport brane
data from L to L′, and obtain a category U.
The invariance of Floer homology under Hamiltonian isotopies implies that, in
a category F which includes both L and U, the objects corresponding to L and L′
are quasi-isomorphic. In particular, we have isomorphisms
HF ∗(L, K) ∼= HF ∗(L′, K) ∼= HF ∗(L′, K′) ∼= HF ∗(L, K′)
(25.13)
for all pairs of objects L, K ∈Ob(L), which are compatible with the multiplication
induced by mF
2 at the cohomological level.

Sketch of proof. The fact that the inclusions induce isomorphisms on Hochschild
homology follows from the more general fact that Hochschild homology is invariant
under triangulated closure. In this special case, we can filter the cyclic bar complex
of F by the number of morphisms belonging, say, to U.
The first step of this
increasing filtration is the cyclic bar complex of U, and the acyclicity of the other
subquotients follows from the acyclicity of the bar complex.
The commutativity of Diagram (25.18) follows from the fact that the choice of
data required to define the map ˆp in Section 15 is inductive on the objects of F. In
particular, having chosen data for U and L, we can extend this data to all elements
of the cyclic bar complex of F, and hence obtain maps which commute at the chain
level.
□

26. Cohomological Smoothness.
26.1. Statement. In this section we prove Theorem 1.16. We define cohomological
smoothness below.
Definition 26.1. Let C is a curvature free A∞category over a field. We consider
the DG category L R(C) of left C right C bimodules. The diagonal module C is its
object. For any pair of objects d1, d2 of C we can define an object Rep(d1, d2) of
L R(C) by
Rep(d1, d2)(c1, c2) = C(c1, d1) ⊗C(d2, c2).


## Page 265


QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY AND SPLIT GENERATION
265
We say C is cohomologically smooth if the diagonal module C is contained in the
full subcategory of L R(C) generated by objects Rep(d1, d2) via the operations:
(i) Degree shift.
(ii) Adding mapping cones of closed morphisms.
(iii) Idempotent closure.
We consider a finite set L consisting of (L, θL) and a finite set L as in Sub-
section 2.11.

Remark 26.2. In case when b = bL = 0 this fact is given in [Fu3, Theorem 3.54]
(using [FOOO7, Theorem 1.4]). This is based on the claim that: by changing JX
to J−X, ωX to −ωX, V (st) to (TX) ⊕V (st) and applying involution τ,132 the

which appears in the process of going from an A∞category to its opposite category.
Actually there is an error in the statement of [Fu3, Theorem 3.54] related to
the sign and orientation. We will explain the error and its correction in Subsection

Remark 26.4.
(1) Actually in [Fu3] only the case bi is a bounding cochain with PO(bi) = 0
is claimed. However as is already explained in [LLL], we can include the
case of weak bounding cochain without changing the proof so much.
(2) We will explain some part of the construction that enters in our proof of
Theorem 26.3 later in Subsection 26.4.
(3) The error mentioned in Remark 26.2 affects the proof of this theorem. The
way to modify the proof is also explained in Subsection 26.7.

26.5. A K¨unneth type homomorphism between Hochschild homologies.
In this section we obtain an element y0 appearing in Proposition 26.17. For this
purpose we will construct a chain map
Φ : CH∗(L; Λ0) ⊗CH∗(L; Λ0) →CH∗(LF; Λ0)
(26.18)
which induces a homomorphism
HH∗(L; Λ0) ⊗HH∗(L; Λ0) →HH∗(LF; Λ0).
(26.19)
Such a homomorphism as (26.19) could be obtained as the composition
HH∗(L; Λ0) ⊗HH∗(L; Λ0) →HH∗(L ⊗L; Λ0)
→HH∗(F; Λ0) →HH∗(LF; Λ0),
(26.20)
where the first homomorphism is an (algebraic) K¨unneth homomorphism, the sec-
ond homomorphism is induced from the A∞functor L⊗L →F constructed in [Fu3,
Theorem 16.9] and the third homomorphism is induced by the Yoneda embedding.
However in this paper we construct (26.18) directly not as the composition
(26.20).
We believe that they coincide.
We however do not prove it since we
do not use it. The particular construction of (26.18) we provide in this subsection
is used for the proof of Proposition 26.17.

We however immediately remark the following. Our discussion on the reflexion
principle is not enough to show Formula (26.40). In fact the reflexion principle
is applied only on a subset where there is no bubble142 and conditions (*), (**)
are satisfied. To prove (26.40) we need to extend it to the compactifications and
find the way to obtain Kuranishi structures and their CF-perturbations (and their
cobordisms) of the compactification so that it is compatible with reflexion.143 We
believe one can do it but to work out its detail is much cumbersome and lengthy.
We therefore take a short cut in this paper to avoid proving (26.40) itself.
We remark that here we only concern with orientations and sign issue. So, for
our purpose, it suffices to compare the local contributions of the left and the right
hand sides of (26.40) at the points where the reflexion principle is applicable. Note
that the maps ZQT and Z are defined by the integration on the moduli spaces.
Therefore the notion of local contributions makes sense.
Moreover in the part
where the reflexion principle is applicable we can compare local contributions.
We recall that the sign for the map Z is given in Definition 20.4 and discussion
thereafter. We also remark that the orientation of the moduli space of the left
hand side is defined by using the reflexion principle. Therefore it is obvious that
we can find the sign ✠in (26.27) uniquely so that (26.40) holds with sign in the
above sense. (Namely local contributions at the point where the reflexion principle
applies coincide together with sign.)
We have thus fixed the sign ✠in (26.27). (We do not provide an explicit formula
since we do not need it.)
Remark 26.35. To prove various properties of ZQT and φ, φH we use the reflexion
principle and reduce the proof to the corresponding statements on Z or m. For
this purpose we need to study certain codimension 1 components, such as the one
with disk bubbles. We can easily observe that to the codimension 1 components
appearing in those discussions, the reflexion principle extends.
We now proceed to the discussion on orientations and signs appearing in the
Cardy relation (Subsubsections 26.6.1 and 26.6.2). We observe that to the moduli
spaces we use there (which are depicted in Figures 79 and 80) we can apply the
reflexion principle in a similar way using appropriate shuffles. So we can prove
a similar formula as (26.40) for Zb
R and ZDSD.

Remark 28.3. In [FOOO4, FOOO6], the bulk deformation is defined by using
equivariant cycles on the toric divisor. In this paper we use differential forms to
define it. However they give the same result as is proved in [FOOO8, Proposition
20.7]. See Subsection 28.5.
Actually even though we use both de Rham model here and in [FOOO4, FOOO6]
the sign conventions may not coincide among them. In [FOOO4, FOOO6] we used
the sign in [FOOO2] (and transform it to differential form). However it is easy to see
that they exactly coincide in case inputs are one form (for forms on L) and two forms
(for forms on X). Therefore the calculation of the leading order term of the potential
function (which we borrow from [CO]) coincides in two convention. Then the rest
of the argument of [FOOO4, FOOO6] uses only the general property of q which
holds for both sign convention. Thus we can prove the results of [FOOO4, FOOO6]
in our sign convention by the same proof.
Remark 28.4. Theorem 1.20 is stated and proved for a toric manifold X. However
the same argument can be used to derive the same conclusion in various other cases,
where we know the number of critical points of the potential function coincides with
the Betti-number, as implemented e.g. in [NNU, CKO, YX].

28.5. Comparison of the perturbations of this paper and of [FOOO6]. In
this paper we construct A∞categories, closed-open maps, and open-closed maps
using Kuranishi structures and CF-perturbations of the moduli spaces.
In this
section we use the result of [FOOO6, Theorem 1.1.1], that is, the Kodaira-Spencer
map from the quantum cohomology of the ambient space to the Jacobian ring is
a ring isomorphism. We use also Kuranishi structures and CF-perturbations in
[FOOO6]. However there are small differences between two constructions, which
we discuss in this subsection.
First we use the divisor axiom (12.21) in [FOOO6]. We can safely do so since
we need it only in the case of finitely many disjoint Lagrangian tori which are
orbits of T n action. To apply our main theorems Theorems 1.7 and 1.8 we need
to consider the case when an ‘unknown’ Lagrangian submanifold U is involved.
So we need to study the case when intersection of U with an orbit is non-trivial.
However the construction of the perturbations for the finite set of Lagrangians, that
is, the union of finitely many orbits and U, is constructed by extending the existing
perturbations for the given finitely many T n orbits. Therefore, the divisor axiom
(12.21) is satisfied as far as finitely many T n orbits we start with concerns.
Another point concerns the way to how to involve bulk deformations. In this
paper we choose differential forms which represent bulk classes. In [FOOO6] we
take singular cycles which are union of irreducible components of toric divisors or
their intersections, and cut the moduli space by requiring the evaluation maps to
hit such cycles. (See [FOOO6, Section 2.3].) We explain that we can still apply
the results ([FOOO6, Theorem 1.1.1]) in our situation to prove (28.7). Here we
compare two potential functions (associated to finitely many T n orbits). Namely:
POb(b) = ⟨mb,b
0 (1), volL⟩cyc ∈Λ0
(28.12)
for the operations mb,b
0
obtained by the two (different) perturbations. Note that
(28.12) is an element of Λ0 which is well defined in the homology level (once a sys-
tem of perturbations is given). Therefore it suffices to compare them modulo T E
for arbitrary but fixed E. To be a bit more detailed, we first observe that the con-
tribution of an element b2 of H2(X; R) is the same for the two perturbations. Note
there is a non trivial relation between cohomology classes represented by irreducible
components of toric divisors. We fix finitely many irreducible components which
generate the subspace isomorphic to H2(X; R). (The proof of the independence of
such choices is a part of [FOOO6, Sections 2.4 and 2.5].) Then we take smooth
closed 2 forms representing a basis of H2(X; R) so that they are smoothing of the
currents corresponding those irreducible components. In particular their supports
are disjoint from the Lagrangian torus involved. By this choice it is easy to see the
contribution is by the cap product β ∩b2 in both cases.
Now it suffices to consider finitely many moduli spaces to compare (28.12) for
two different choices of perturbations. The argument leading to a proof of such
coincidence is the same as [FOOO8, Section 28.3].
The third point we need to discuss is that the curved A∞categories (bulk de-
formed by the same class b) are homotopy equivalent when we use two different


## Page 307


QUANTUM COHOMOLOGY AND SPLIT GENERATION
307
perturbations. Namely the part which does not contain T and of degree 0 contribute
in the same way. Then we can use [FOOO8, Section 28.3] to compare the bulk de-
formation using irreducible components of toric divisors or their intersections and
one by using their smoothing (degree two forms).
We remark that using the perturbations of [FOOO8] it is proved that any coho-
mology class b1 ∈H1(L; Λ0) gives rise to a bounding cochain [FOOO3, Proposition
4.3]. If we use the bulk deformation by differential forms (the ones we use here), this
may not be the case.145 In fact the curved A∞algebras (H∗(L; Λ0), mb) obtained by
these two different types of bulk deformations are homotopy equivalent. However
the homotopy equivalence may not preserve the Z grading. Therefore the set of
bounding cochains may not coincide with the cohomology group H1(L; Λ0) under
the bulk deformation we use in this paper, although it is identified with H1(L; Λ0)
for the toric case. Neverthless there is still an embedding H1(L; Λ0) ,→Mweak(L)
since Mweak(L) is invariant under the homotopy equivalence. We can therefore use
the result of [FOOO3].

Let ea be the unit of the factor QHb(X; Λ)a. It is easy to see that the decomposition

We take a finite set L of pairs (L, b) of Lagrangian submanifolds L and weak
bounding cochains b. Let L be the cyclic A∞category the set of whose object is
L. We then define the associated cyclic A∞category Dπ(L) as in the introduction.
We say an object c of Dπ(L) is indecomposable if the idempotent of HF(c, c) is the
unit or 0.
Remark 30.2. For a pair (L, b) of Lagrangian submanifold and its weak bounding
cochain, it frequently happens that it decomposes into finitely many mutually iso-
morphic objects. For example sometimes HF((L, b), (L, b); ΛC) is a matrix algebra
M(N). (In the case of a T n orbit of a toric manifold, for example, its self-Floer ho-
mology is a Clifford algebra. Sometimes it is a matrix algebra.) In such a case (L, b)
is isomorphic to the direct sum of N copies of indecomposable objects. Proposition
30.3 below applies to such (L, b) since direct summands are mutually isomorphic.
It is easy to see that any object of Dπ(L) is a direct sum of indecomposable
objects.
If c is indecomposable, then there exists a unique λ ∈Λ0 such that

Remark 30.11.
(1) In the case X = T 2, the conclusion of Theorem 30.10 does not hold. Simi-
larly it does not hold in many of Calabi-Yau manifolds. In fact in many such
cases we find infinitely many Lagrangian tori which are mutually disjoint
and have nontrivial Floer homology.
For example let us consider the case X = T 2n = Cn/Zn and Li =
T n = Rn/Zn + ai
√−1.
(ai ∈Rn).
In this case ˆp : HF(Li, Li; Λ) →
H(X; Λ) coincides with Gysin map Hk(T n; Λ) →Hk+n(T 2n; Λ). Therefore
obviously
⟨bp(xi),bp(xj)⟩PDX = 0,
for xi ∈HF(Li, Li; Λ), xj ∈HF(Lj, Lj; Λ). Moreover the open closed map
bp (defined on the Hochschild homology of the A∞category whose objects
are Lagrangian T n as above) never hits eX ∈H0(X).
(2) In [FOOO8, Corollary 24.4] we proved that the conclusion of Theorem
30.10 does not hold for the cubic surface and b = 0. In fact the quantum
cohomology of cubic surface is not semi-simple.
(3) In case X is S2 × S2 or certain k ≥2 points toric blow up of CP 2, we
find infinitely many Lagrangian tori that are mutually disjoint and have
nontrivial Floer homology ([FOOO4, FOOO5]). However their quantum
cohomology are semi-simple. In those examples, we varied the bulk class b
to obtain nontrivial Floer homology. So Theorem 30.10 does not apply to
that case.
Let L ⊂X be a Lagrangian submanifold. We take its Weinstein neighborhood
and take a family L(u) of Lagrangian submanifolds parametrized by u in a small
neighborhood of 0 in H1(L; R). Namely L(u) is the graph of a closed one form
representing u. (Here we identify the Weinstein neighborhood with a neighborhood
of zero section in T ∗L.) The Lagrangian L(u) is unique up to Hamiltonian isotopy
supported in the Weinstein neighborhood.
Conjecture 30.12. Suppose QHb(X; Λ) is semi-simple. Then there exist only a
finite number of u that enjoy the following property:
There exists b ∈Mweak(L(u); b) such that
HF((L(u), b), (L(u), b); Λ) ̸= 0.
Theorem 30.10 implies Conjecture 30.12 in case when there exists a fiber bundle
π : L →T b such that b = rank H1(L; R) and that the first Betti number of the
fiber is zero. In fact, in such case, we may take L(u) so that L(u) ∩L(u′) = ∅for
u ̸= u′. The general case seems to be related to the Lagrangian version of the Flux
conjecture. (See [On1].)

The results of this section are mostly concerned with the case when quantum
cohomology is close to be semi-simple.

c1(X) = 0, the situation is different.

By Theorem 31.1, (30.18) also follows from the following assumption.

We point out that there is an example of Lagrangian submanifold in K3 surface
where (a)(b)(c) above hold but (ii) does not hold. This suggests that Conjecture
30.17 is not so obvious.
To construct such an example we study Kummer surface below. We consider
X0 = T 4/{±1} where −1 acts by [x1, . . . , x4] 7→[−x1, . . . , −x4].

symlectic form ω on it that is induced by an invariant symplectic form on T 4. X0

have 16 singular points of type A1 (that is locally R4/{±1}). We replace small
neighborhoods of those 16 points by the Milnor fiber that is symplectomorphic to a
neighborhood of zero section in T ∗S2. We thus obtain a smooth symplectic manifold
X, that is actually a Kummer surface. (It contains 16 Lagrangian spheres.)
T ∗S2 contains a family of Lagrangian submanifolds T(u), that we describe below.
For u = 1 we take L(1) ⊂T ∗S2 as the union of unit tangent vector of closed
geodesics of S2 which contains both north and south poles. (See [FOOO5, appendix
2].) (Here we identify TS2 with T ∗S2 using the standard metric of S2.)
For general u we put L(u) = {uv | v ∈L(1)}.
We may identify a small neighborhood of zero section in T ∗S2 to an open set
in X.
Therefore L(u) may be regarded as a Lagrangian submanifold of X for
sufficiently small u, that we write T(u). (Actually there are 16 different choices of
T(u) for given u.)
It is easy to see that the minimal Maslov number of T(u) is 2 and is not 0. On
the other hand, we have the following:
Proposition 30.18. If u is sufficiently small then there exists b(u) ∈H2(X; Λ0)
and b(u) ∈H1(T(u); Λ0) such that
HF((T(u), (b(u), b(u))), (T(u), (b(u), b(u))); Λ0) = H(T 2; Λ0).
Remark 30.19.
(1) Since dim X = 4 we have
H1(T(u); Λ0) ⊂Mweak(L(u); b; Λ0),
by [FOOO5, Theorem A.1].)
(2) (30.18) holds in our case, since (a), (b), (c) above are satisfied.
Proof of Proposition 30.18. We take compatible almost complex structure JU on
U so that JU is exactly the same as one used in [FOOO5, Section 6]. We choose an
almost complex structure J on X so that there is no J-holomorphic disk bounding
T(u) with Maslov index ≤0 and J coincides with JU on U. (We can prove it by
using starndard dimension counting argument.)
Let β ∈Π2(X; T(u)) be an element of Maslov index 2. We define the number
nβ as the degree of the evaluation map
M1(T(u); β) →T(u).
The degree is well-defined since there is no disk with Maslov number ≤0. (We
remark that nβ may depend on the choice of almost complex structure J, however.)
By [FOOO5, Section 6] there are four classes βi ∈Π2(X; T(u)), i = 1, . . . , 4 such
that nβi = 1 and βi is realized by a holomorphic disk contained in U. Moreover
βi ∩ω = u. There is a positive number independent of u such that if β ̸= βi and if
nβ ̸= 0 then β ∩ω > λ. (This is a consequence of the fact that a J-disk realizing
the class β cannot be contained in U.)
We may choose the basis of H1(T(u); Z) such that
∂β1 = (1, 0),
∂β2 = ∂β3 = (0, −1),
∂β4 = (−1, −2).

Remark 31.3. We remark that the condition b ∈H1(L; Λ0) depends on vari-
ous choices in general. Note that we have c
Mweak(L; Λ0) ⊂Hodd(L; Λ0), and that
the moduli space c
Mweak(L; Λ0) of bounding cochains is independent of the choices.
Precisely speaking, this independence means the following. Let J1, J2 be two differ-
ent choices of compatible almost complex structures (we also need to fix Kuranishi
structure and CF-perturbation also, but we do not include them in the notation).
We then define c
Mweak(L; Λ0; Ji) as a subset of the cohomology groups of odd de-
grees. Now there exists a map
Ψ : Hodd(L; Λ0) →Hodd(L; Λ0)
that is non-linear and does not preserve degree in general such that
c
Mweak(L; Λ0; J2) = Ψ( c
Mweak(L; Λ0; J1)).
Thus the condition b ∈H1(L; Λ0) depends on the choices and is not an intrinsic
condition. In the following cases this condition becomes more reasonable:
(1) If dimC X = 2 then Hodd(L; Λ0) = H1(L; Λ0).
Hence the condition is
automatically satisfied.
(2) Let L be a fiber of toric manifold. We proved in [FOOO3, FOOO4] that
H1(L; Λ0) ⊂c
Mweak(L; Λ0) in case we take the almost complex structure,
the Kuranishi structure, and the multisection to be T n equivariant.
So
the condition holds in this case, if we reformulate the story using singular
homology rather than de Rham cohomology.
(3) If L is the fixed locus of an anti-symplectic involution, then we can take
an involution anti-invariant almost complex structure, and appropriately
equivariant Kuranishi structures and multisections. In this case b = 0 is a
good candidate for the choice of c
Mweak(L; Λ0).
(4) If L is monotone again b = 0 is an element of c
Mweak(L; Λ0).

of the third line is a consequence of Theorem 17.1. The equality of the fourth line
is a consequence of Proposition 31.4. The equality of the fifth line is a consequence
of cyclic symmetry. We have thus proved (31.3).
To study the general case we consider the decomposition (30.2). Since c1(X) has
even degree it is in the center of QHb(X; Λ). Therefore each space
QHb(X; Λ)ρ = {x ∈QHb(X; Λ) | ∃N, (c1(X) −ρ)Nx = 0}
is a direct product factor of QHb(X; Λ). Therefore each direct factor QHb(X; Λ)a
in the decomposition (30.2) is contained in one of the generalized eigenspaces
QHb(X; Λ)ρ.
We decompose Dπ(L) into the product of Dπ(L; a) as in (30.3). Then Lemma
30.4 implies that
ˆpb(Dπ(L; a)) ⊂QHb(X; Λ)a.
Therefore
ˆpb(Dπ(L; a)) ⊂QHb(X; Λ)ρ,
for some ρ.
By (31.3) ρ = λ if Dπ(L; a) ̸= 0.
The proof of Corollary 31.5 is
complete.
□
We remark that Corollary 31.5 implies Theorem 1.7 in case the assumption of
Theorem 31.1 is satisfied.

Then there exists a non-constant holomorphic map u : (D2, ∂D2) →(CP n, U) such
that

Proof. Let L0 ⊂CP n be the Clifford torus.
We take b = 0.
We put χk =

(k = 0, . . . , n) for the unit of k-th factor. We may choose them so that
q(ek; 1L0) ̸= 0 ∈HF((L0, bk), (L0, bk); Λ).

Since PO(bk) is not congruent to 0 modulo T 1/(n+1), this is a contradiction.
